be a doubly infinite sequence with the following properties:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}=\infty,\quad\lim_{n\to-\infty}\tau_{n}=-\infty,\text{ and }\tau_{-n}<0<\tau_{n}\text{ for all }n\geq 1.

(2.3.1)

For brevity, we will say that
\left\{\tau_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
sequence of admissible heights
.

Languidity consists of two hypotheses, the first of which concerns power-law/polynomial growth along a sequence of horizontal lines. The horizontal lines each pass from a point on a screen
S
to some point in a half-plane in which the function is holomorphic.

Definition 2.3.1

(Languidity Hypothesis
L1
)
.

We say that
f
satisfies languidity hypothesis
L1
with exponent
\kappa=0
and with respect to the screen
S
if the following hold.

Firstly, there must exist some half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{D}
in which
f
is holomorphic and
f
must admit an extension to the screen
S
. Secondly, there must exist a positive constant
C>0
, a constant
\beta>D
, and a sequence of admissible heights
\left\{\tau_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
(in the sense of Equation
2.3.1
) such that for any
\sigma\in[S(\tau_{n}),\beta]
,

|f(\sigma+i\tau_{n})|\leq C(|\tau_{n}|+1)^{\kappa}.

In other words, on every horizontal contour of the form
I_{n}=[S(\tau_{n})+i\tau_{n},\,\beta+i\tau_{n}]
,
f
has at most power-law
\kappa
growth with respect to the magnitude of its imaginary part
\tau_{n}
as
|\tau_{n}|\to\infty
. Alternatively, on each horizontal contour
I_{n}
,
f
is uniformly bounded by the constant
C_{n}
, where
C_{n}=O((|\tau_{n}|+1)^{\kappa})
as
|n|\to\infty
.

The second hypothesis concerns power-law/polynomial growth along a vertical curve, the screen
S
. The standard (or weak) version of languidity concerns growth along a single curve, and strong languidity concerns growth along a sequence of such curves moving to the left. These curves are precisely the screens of the window(s) to which
f
admits an extension.

Definition 2.3.2

(Languidity Hypothesis
L2
)
.

A function
f
is said to satisfy languidity hypothesis
L2
with exponent
\kappa
and with respect to the screen
S
if there exists a positive constant
C>0
such that for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
with
|\tau|\geq 1
,
|f(S(\tau)+i\tau)|\leq C|\tau|^{\kappa}
.

A
function
which satisfies both hypotheses
L1
and
L2
with respect to the same screen
S
and exponent
\kappa
is said to be languid, or equivalently to have languid growth. A geometric structure (such as a generalized fractal harp, a relative fractal drum, or a self-similar system) may be called languid if its associated zeta function (respectively, its geometric zeta function, its relative zeta function, or its scaling zeta function) is languid, i.e. it satisfies languidity hypotheses
L1
and
L2
.

Definition 2.3.3

((Standard/Weak) Languidity)
.

We say that
f
is
languid
with exponent
\kappa
if there exists a screen
S
such that
f
satisfies both languidity hypotheses
L1
(Definition
2.3.1
) and
L2
(Definition
2.3.2
) with exponent to
\kappa
and with respect to the screen
S
.

Given a particular screen
S
, we say that a function
f
is languid
with respect to

S
(with exponent
\kappa
) if it satisfies these hypotheses (with exponent
\kappa
) with respect to the given screen.

We will not have use of this presently, but one may also say that a function
f
is languid if there exists some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and some screen
S
for which
f
is languid with exponent
\kappa
and with respect to
S
.

2.3.2
Strong Languidity

Strong languidity may be seen as a sequence of languidity conditions. Namely, it concerns languidity along
a sequence of screens

\left\{S_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

converging to

-\infty
. To be more precise, let
\sup S
denote the supremum of the elements of the range of
S
, i.e.
\sup S:=\sup\left\{S(\tau)\,\middle|\,\tau\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
. Then a sequence of screens
\left\{S_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is said to converge to
-\infty
if
\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup{S_{m}}=-\infty
.

Additionally, we will impose that the sequence of screens has
uniformly bounded Lipschitz constants
. For a given screen
S_{m}
, which is by definition Lipschitz continuous, the
Lipschitz constant

K_{m}
is the smallest constant such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
|S_{m}(x)-S_{m}(y)|\leq K_{m}|x-y|
. Then to say that the Lipschitz constants of a sequence of screens
\left\{S_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded means that there exists a constant
K
such that for every Lipschitz constant
K_{m}
of
S_{m}
,
K_{m}\leq K
. (In other words, the set of all Lipschitz constants,
\left\{K_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded by some finite constant
K
.)

Definition 2.3.4

(Strong Languidity of a Function)
.

Let
f
be a holomorphic function possessing an analytic continuation to the whole complex plane except possibly for a discrete set of singular points. Then
f
is said to be
strongly languid
with exponent
\kappa
if there exists a sequence
\left\{S_{m}\right\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of screens converging to
-\infty
with uniformly bounded Lipschitz constants such that:

L1

f
satisfies languidity hypothesis
L1
with respect to each screen
S_{m}
and the (fixed) constant
\kappa
. (Equivalently, with respect to the replacement
S(\tau)\equiv-\infty
.)

L2
′

There exist positive constants
C
and
B
such that for all
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\geq 1
,
f
satisfies
|f(S_{m}(\tau)+i\tau)|\leq CB^{|S_{m}(\tau)|}(|\tau|+1)^{\kappa}
.

Note that the strong languidity condition allows for a prefactor with exponential growth related to
\left\lVert S_{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}
, but is otherwise analogous to
L2
. Condition
L2
′
implies
L2
for each of the screens with finite supremum. Also, without loss of generality, the constant
C
may be chosen to be the same in both hypotheses.

2.3.3
Strong Languidity of Self-Similar Systems

Self-similar systems and their associated scaling zeta functions will play a critical role in the analysis to follow. As it will turn out, a self-similar system is always strongly languid in the sense that its associated scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
is strongly languid with exponent
\kappa
. We first state this for scaling operators having positive, integral multiplicities and scaling ratios in
(0,1)
.

Proposition 2.3.5

(Languidity of Scaling Zeta Functions)
.

Let
L:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}M_{\lambda_{i}}
be scaling operator with distinct scaling ratios
\lambda_{i}\in(0,1)
and positive integral multiplicities
a_{i}
. Then its associated scaling zeta function
\zeta_{L}
is strongly languid with respect to exponent
\kappa=0
as in Definition
2.3.4
.

Proof 2.3.6
.

This result is a corollary of Theorem 3.26 and the discussion in Section 6.4 of
[
LvF13
]
, as
\zeta_{L}
is essentially the same as that of a self-similar fractal string (or harp). (Let the length and gap parameters be one.) Note in particular the explicit estimate in Equation 6.36 of
[
LvF13
]
used to establish the uniform bounds on screens with arbitrarily small real parts.

For a self-similar system
\Phi
, its associated scaling operator
L_{\Phi}
will always have scaling ratios in
(0,1)
and positive, integral multiplicities. Thus, it is an immediate corollary of Proposition
2.3.5
that the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}=\zeta_{L_{\Phi}}
is strongly languid. Extending the convention that a geometric structure itself may be dubbed languid when its associated zeta function is languid, we may say that self-similar systems are languid.

Corollary 2.3.7

(Languiditiy of Self-Similar Systems)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then
\Phi
is strongly languid (with exponent
\kappa=0
) in the sense that its associated scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
is strongly languid (with exponent
\kappa=0
) as in Definition
2.3.4
.

2.3.4
Joint Languidity

In what follows, we will require the existence of screens
S
for which two functions
f
and
g
are both languid with respect to the
same
screen
S
. We shall call this
joint languidity
.

As a preliminary, we define an open right
half-plane of maximal joint extension
for two holomorphic functions
f
and
g
: it is a half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
, for some
\sigma_{0}\in[-\infty,\infty)
, such that
f
and
g
each admit an analytic continuation to
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
(except perhaps for a discrete subset of singularities) and with the property that for any other half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
with this property,
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
. (This is equivalent to imposing
\sigma_{0}\leq\sigma_{1}
, however one may define a more general type of domain of maximal joint extension where containment is the appropriate condition.)

If we assume that there is some half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
in which
f
and
g
are both holomorphic, then a half-plane of maximal joint extension always exists. It may be
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
or else it must contain
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
. If a half-plane of maximal joint extension exists, it is necessarily unique. (Any two candidates must contain the other as a subset, which implies that they are the same set.)

Definition 2.3.8

(Joint Languidity)
.

Let
f
and
g
be functions which are both holomorphic in some half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{1}}
and let
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
be their half-plane of maximal joint extension.

We say that
f
and
g
are
jointly languid
with exponent
\kappa=0
if there exists a screen
S
contained in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
such that the following hold.

•

There is a sequence of admissible heights for which
f
and
g
both satisfy languidity hypothesis
L1
with exponent
\kappa=0
and with respect to
S
using this sequence.

•

Both
f
and
g
satisfy languidity hypothesis
L2
with exponent
\kappa=0
and with respect to
S
.

2.4
Scaling Functional Equations

In order to treat
tube functions
(see Chapter
3
) and
heat content
(see Chapter
4
) in a unified way, we introduce a general framework to describe the nature of both of these functions simultaneously. Each of these quantities is a function
f_{X}(t)=f(t;X)
depending on a set
X
and a (nonnegative) parameter
t
. When we consider a self-similar set
X
, arising as the attractor of self-similar system
\Phi
, we can typically express
f_{X}
in terms of the functions
f_{\varphi[X]}
, with
\varphi\in X
. The key idea is that, after an appropriate normalization of the quantity (e.g. volume or heat content), we obtain a scaling relation of the form
f(t;\varphi[X])=f(t/\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha};X)
, where
\alpha
is fixed (depending on the context, and in this work either
\alpha=1
or
\alpha=2
) and
\lambda_{\varphi}
is the scaling ratio of
\varphi
.

So, if the self-similar system
\Phi

induces a decomposition
of the form

f(t;X)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}f(t;\varphi[X])+R(t),

then we may rewrite each
f(t;\varphi[X])
in terms of a single function,
f_{X}
, alone but with a rescaled input. This leads to a new expression,

f_{X}(t)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}f_{X}(t/\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha})+R(t),

which is exactly a
scaling functional equation
(with an error term
R
) induced by
\Phi
. Defining the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
, this equation may be written as
f_{X}=L_{\varphi}^{\alpha}[f_{X}]+R
.

2.4.1
Induced Decompositions and Scaling Functional Equations

In our applications, we will have a self-similar system
\Phi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and a collection of functions
f_{A}
from a subset of
\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbb{R}
.
A
will either be a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
or else a relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. In what follows, we will give meaning to the notion of a self-similar system
inducing a scaling functional equation
in a theoretical manner. The idea is that this section outlines this approach for studying more general sorts of functions indexed by some collection of geometric or algebraic objects.

The terminology is generalized from the situations which we will see in Chapter
3
and Chapter
4
and treats them in a unified way. Further, it suggests how to proceed in an arbitrary context: find a scaling law, which may depend on some parameter (or even a more general sort of transformation), as well as a decomposition rule, and then a scaling functional equation will appear.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
(or, more generally, on a metric space) and let
\mathcal{A}
be a collection of objects with the following closure property: if
A\in\mathcal{A}
, then
\varphi[A]
is well-defined and
\varphi[A]\in\mathcal{A}
for each
\varphi\in\Phi
. For brevity, we will say that
\mathcal{A}
is
\Phi
-closed. Next, let
\left\{f_{A}\right\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}}
be a family of functions
f_{A}:I\to G
for some set
I
and an additive semigroup
(G,+)
. We will identify
f
with the family and write
f(t;A)=f_{A}(t)
for
t\in I
and
A\in\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.4.1

(Induced Decomposion)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Phi
-closed collection of objects, and let
f=\left\{f_{A}\right\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}}
be a family of functions
f_{A}:I\to G
for some set
I
and an additive semigroup
(G,+)
. Let also
R:I\to G
be a function.

A self-similar system
\Phi
is said to
induce a decomposition
of
f
on
I
with remainder
R
if for all
t\in I
,

f(t;A)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}f(t;\varphi[A])+R(t).

(2.4.1)

In this work,
I
is always a subset of
\mathbb{R}
and the codomain is the field
\mathbb{R}
with its standard addition operation. Further,
A
is some geometric structure, either a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
or a relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
. If
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\varphi[A]
is its pointwise image. For a relative fractal drum, we define
\varphi[(X,\Omega)]:=(\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega])
, noting that this also defines a relative fractal drum.

An induced decomposition will be paired with a scaling law satisfied by the function
f(t;A)
. A linear scaling (invariance) law would be of the form
f(\lambda t;\lambda A)=f(t;A)
. However, depending on the context, the real and set parameters may scale differently. For example, heat contents (see Chapter
4
) will obey a quadratic scaling law:
f(\lambda^{2}\,t;\lambda A)=f(t;A)
. We provide a general notion for defining the scaling relation which allows one to specify the parameter
\alpha
dictating this relationship.

Definition 2.4.2

(
\alpha
-Scaling Law)
.

Let
f=\left\{f(\cdot;A)\right\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}}
be a family of functions
f_{A}:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
where
\mathcal{A}
is a
\left\{\varphi\right\}
-closed collection of objects for any similitude
\varphi
of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

We say that
f
has an
\alpha
-scaling law
if for any similitude
\varphi
with scaling ratio
\lambda>0
and for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
f(t;\varphi[A])=f(t/\lambda^{\alpha};A)
.

We note that this equation could alternatively have been written as
f(\lambda^{\alpha}t;\varphi[A])=f(t;A)
. If we were to define the action of
\varphi
on the family
f
by
\varphi[f_{A}]:=f_{\varphi[A]}\circ(t\mapsto\lambda^{\alpha}t)
, then this equation says that each function is invariant with respect to this action, viz.
\varphi[f_{A}]=f_{A}
for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
and similitude
\varphi
. However, the way we have written will be more useful for rewriting induced decompositions. In this form, the equation is more akin to a type of equivariance:
f_{\varphi[A]}(t)=f_{A}(\varphi*t)
, where
\varphi*t:=t/\lambda^{\alpha}
is an action on
(\mathbb{R}^{+},\cdot)
.

We conclude this section with the main idea: a scaling law and an induced decomposition for a family of functions together lead to the scaling functional equation of a single function.

Proposition 2.4.3

(Scaling Functional Equation (SFE))
.

Let
f=\left\{f_{A}\right\}_{A\in\mathcal{A}}
be a family of functions on
I
satisfying an
\alpha
-scaling law. Suppose that
\Phi
is a self-similar system which induces a decomposition of
f
on
I
with remainder term
R
. Define the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
by
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
.

Then each function
f_{A}
in the family satisfies the
scaling function equation

f_{A}=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f_{A}]+R
on
I
with error term
R
, which is to say that for all
t\in I
,

f_{A}(t)=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f_{A}](t)+R(t).

We say that
\Phi
induces a scaling functional equation for
f_{A}
with operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
.

Proposition
2.4.3
is, in essence, an untangling of definitions meant to lead to a single key concept:
a self-similar system induces a scaling functional equation
for functions with scaling laws. It is, however, a consequence of the definitions that any function in this family satisfies an induced scaling functional equation. The proof is merely to apply the
\alpha
-scaling law to each term of an induced decomposition and then use the definition of the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
to rewrite the decomposition in terms of a single function.

In general, any function
f
which satisfies an equation of the form
f=L[f]+R
for some scaling operator
L
is said to satisfy a scaling functional equation (with error
R
). If
R\equiv 0
, the scaling functional equation is said to be exact, and if not then it is said to be an approximate scaling functional equation.

2.4.2
Admissible Remainders

As part of our analysis of scaling functional equations, we will be required to show that certain functions are languid (see Section
2.3
). In so doing, we will need to constrain the types of remainders which can appear in order for these results to be established. In this section, we introduce terminology for such
admissible remainders
relative to a given self-similar system as well as some sufficient conditions for admissibility.

The admissibility of a remainder is essentially tied to a need for
joint languidity
. Two functions, the Mellin transform of the remainder
\zeta_{R}
and the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
, must share the same screen and the same horizontal contours on which they are both uniformly bounded. They must both be languid (with exponent
\kappa
) on a shared screen. It is for this reason that we introduced the notion of
joint languidity
(Definition
2.3.8
).

Definition 2.4.4

(Admissible Remainders and Screens)
.

Let
R
be a function such that its truncated Mellin transform
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)
is holomorphic in some half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
. This occurs, for instance, if
R
is continuous on
(0,\delta]
and
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
(per Lemma
2.1.2
).

Let
L
be a scaling operator with associated scaling zeta function
\zeta_{L}
. We say that
R
is an
admissible remainder
for
L
if there exists a screen
S
such that
\zeta_{L}
and
\zeta_{R}
are jointly languid with exponent
\kappa=0
with respect to
S
in the sense of Definition
2.3.8
. Any such screen
S
is called an
admissible screen
for
R
and
L
(or their respective zeta functions).

Given a self-similar system
\Phi
, we say that
R
is an admissible remainder for
\Phi
if it is admissible for the associated operator
L_{\Phi}
(and similarly for admissible screens).

The easiest way to obtain a nontrivial admissible screen occurs when
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in a half-plane to the left of the vertical strip containing all of the poles of
\zeta_{L}
. This ensures that a screen may be chosen to the left of any pole of
\zeta_{L}
but also in a region where
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic. Supposing that
L=L_{\Phi}
is the scaling operator associated to a self-similar system, we have that this strip is bounded explicitly by the similarity dimensions of
\Phi
: if
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
, then
\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)\leq\text{Re}(\omega)\leq\dim_{S}(\Phi)
. So, the first criterion is to ensure an estimate for
R
that guarantees its abscissa of holomorphic convergence is strictly smaller than the lower similarity dimension.

Theorem 2.4.5

(Lower Dimension Criterion for Admissibility)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and let
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
be its lower similarity dimension (see Definition
2.2.9
). For any
R\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
, if there exists
\sigma_{0}<D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
such that as
t\to 0^{+}
,
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
, then
R
is an admissible remainder for
\Phi
and any screen of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon
, with
0<\varepsilon<D_{\ell}-\sigma_{0}
, is admissible.

Proof 2.4.6
.

Let
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
be the lower similarity dimension of
\Phi
and let
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon
be a constant screen in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
, where
\varepsilon>0
is such that
\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon<D_{\ell}
. We will show that
\zeta_{R}
and
\zeta_{\Phi}
are jointly languid with exponent
\kappa=0
on
S_{\varepsilon}
.

To start, we have that
\zeta_{\Phi}
is strongly languid with exponent
\kappa=0
by Corollary
2.3.7
. This guarantees that for any
\sigma_{-}
, there exists of a sequence of admissible heights
\left\{T_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
(i.e. with
T_{-n}\to-\infty
and
T_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and with
T_{n}>0>T_{-n}
for each
n\geq 1
) and some screen
S_{-}
with
\sup S_{-}<\sigma_{-}
such that
\zeta_{\Phi}
is uniformly bounded on horizontal contours of the form
[S_{-}(T_{n})+iT_{n},\sigma_{+}+iT_{n}]
, where
\sigma_{+}>\max(\sigma_{-},\dim_{S}(\Phi))
. In particular,
\zeta_{\Phi}
will be uniformly bounded on the subsets
I_{n}:=[\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon+iT_{n},\sigma_{+}+iT_{n}]
. This establishes languidity hypothesis
L1
for
\zeta_{\Phi}
.

Next, we note that the estimate on
R
implies that the function
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in the open right half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
by Lemma
2.1.2
. By Corollary
2.1.4
, we also have that
\zeta_{R}
is bounded on the screen
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon>\sigma_{0}
as well as on any vertical strip of the form
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}^{\sigma_{+}}
. This establishes both languidity hypotheses
L1
and
L2
with exponent
\kappa=0
and on the screen
S_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the intervals
I_{n}\subset\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}^{\sigma_{+}}
, with shared sequence of admissible heights.

It remains to show that
\zeta_{\Phi}
is bounded on the screen
S_{\varepsilon}
. To this end, we enforce that
\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon<D_{\ell}
, in which case for any
s
on the screen,
\text{Re}(s)=\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon<D_{\ell}
. Let
\left\{r_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{m}
denote the set of unique scaling ratios of
\Phi
, arranged in decreasing order
r_{1}\geq...\geq r_{M}
, and let
m_{k}
denote the multiplicity of
r_{k}
. Define the function

p(t)=\frac{1}{m_{M}}(r_{M}^{-1})^{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{M-1}\frac{m_{k}}{m_{M}}\left(\frac{r_{k}}{r_{M}}\right)^{t},

(2.4.2)

which is readily seen to be strictly increasing with range
(0,\infty)
. Note that
p(D_{\ell})=1
by definition. We will obtain a bound for
\zeta_{\Phi}(s)
when
\text{Re}(s)=\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon<D_{\ell}
using this function.

To that end, let
f(s)=\zeta_{\Phi}(s)^{-1}=1-\sum_{k=1}^{M}m_{k}r_{k}^{s}
be the denominator. Then

\displaystyle|r_{M}^{-s}/m_{M}f(s)+1|

\displaystyle=\left|r_{M}^{-s}/m_{M}-\sum_{k=1}^{M-1}m_{k}/m_{M}(r_{k}/r_{M})^{s}\right|

\displaystyle\leq p(\sigma)<p(D_{\ell})=1.

By the reverse triangle inequality, we have that

\displaystyle 1>p(\sigma)\geq\left||r_{M}^{-s}/m_{M}f(s)|-1\right|=\left|r_{M}^{-\sigma}/m_{M}|f(s)|-1\right|,

which may be rewritten as
||f(s)|-m_{M}r^{\sigma}|\leq m_{M}r^{\sigma}p(\sigma)<m_{M}r_{M}^{\sigma}
. Once again using the reverse triangle inequality, we find that the lower bound for
|f|
is furnished by

\displaystyle|f(s)|\geq m_{M}r_{M}^{\sigma}-||f(s)|-m_{M}r^{\sigma}|\geq m_{M}r_{M}^{\sigma}-m_{M}r^{\sigma}p(\sigma)>0.

It follows that when
\text{Re}(s)=\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon
is fixed,
|\zeta_{\Phi}(s)|\leq C_{\Phi}
, with
C_{\Phi}=(m_{M}r_{M}^{\sigma}-m_{M}r^{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}p(\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon))^{-1}
. This establishes hypothesis
L2
for
\zeta_{\Phi}
, and thus
\zeta_{\Phi}
and
\zeta_{R}
are jointly languid on the screen
S_{\varepsilon}
.

In order to accommodate
\alpha
-scaling laws with
\alpha>0
(in the sense of Definition
2.4.2
), we will consider scaling operators of the form
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
, where
\Phi
is a self-similar system. In this case, the zeta function associated to
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
is exactly the function
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
, where
\zeta_{\Phi}(s)
is the scaling zeta function associated to
\Phi
itself.

This change of variables amounts to a simple rescaling of the bound with respect to the lower similarity dimension of
\Phi
a remainder must satisfy in order to be admissible by Theorem
2.4.5
. Namely, when
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
for some
\sigma_{R}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)/\alpha
, we have that
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
has poles in the strip
\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)/\alpha\leq\text{Re}(s)\leq\dim_{S}(\Phi)/\alpha
and
\sigma_{R}
lies strictly to the left of this bound. The estimates for
\zeta_{\Phi}
apply to its rescaled analogue, and thus proof of Theorem
2.4.5
yields the following corollary.

Corollary 2.4.7

(Rescaled Lower Dimension Criterion)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and let
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
be its lower similarity dimension (see Definition
2.2.9
). Given
\alpha>0
, define the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
.

For any
R\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
, if there exists
\sigma_{R}<D_{\ell}/\alpha
such that as
t\to 0^{+}
,
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
, then
R
is an admissible remainder for
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
and any screen of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon
, with
0<\varepsilon<D_{\ell}/\alpha-\sigma_{R}
, is admissible.

Proof 2.4.8
.

By Lemma
2.1.2
, we have that
\zeta_{R}(s)
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
and thus
\zeta_{R}(z/\alpha)
is holomorphic when
\text{Re}(z)>\alpha\,\sigma_{R}
. Further,
\zeta_{R}(s)
is bounded on any vertical strip of the form
\mathbb{H}_{a}^{b}
,
\sigma_{R}<a\leq b<\infty
, so
\zeta_{R}(z/\alpha)
is bounded on the corresponding strips
\mathbb{H}_{a^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}}
,
\alpha\,\sigma_{R}<a^{\prime}\leq b^{\prime}<\infty
.

Since
\alpha\,\sigma_{R}<D_{\ell}
, by repeating the proof of Theorem
2.4.5
(with
\sigma_{0}=\alpha\,\sigma_{R}
) we obtain that
\zeta_{\Phi}(z)
and
\zeta_{R}(z/\alpha)
are jointly languid on screens of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\alpha\,\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon
when
0<\varepsilon<D_{\ell}-\alpha\,\sigma_{R}
. Taking
z=\alpha s
, we see that the functions
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s)
are jointly languid on screens of the form
S^{\prime}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon^{\prime}
where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/\alpha\in(0,D_{\ell}/\alpha-\sigma_{R})
.

The next criterion is related to when we have explicit knowledge of the locations of the singularities of
\zeta_{\Phi}
. In particular, in the lattice case (see Definition
1.1.4
), we can explicitly show that all of the poles lie on one of finitely many vertical lines (and are distributed periodically along these lines with a shared period for each line). Thus, we can easily choose screens within this region which will never encounter singularities of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, with distance to any pole bounded by the distance of the real part of the screen to the real part of the closest of the finitely many exceptional points.

Theorem 2.4.9

(Lattice Criterion for Admissiblility)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and suppose that its scaling ratios
\left\{\lambda_{\varphi}\right\}_{\varphi\in\Phi}
are arithmetically related (see Definition
1.1.4
). Let
R
be a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with the estimate that
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
for some
\sigma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then for all but finitely many
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
,
S_{\sigma}(\tau)\equiv\sigma
is an admissible screen. Consequently, there are admissible screens of the form
S_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Proof 2.4.10
.

By definition, in the lattice case there exists some
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for each
\varphi\in\Phi
, there is a positive integer
k_{\varphi}
such that
\lambda_{\varphi}=\lambda_{0}^{k_{\varphi}}
. Under this assumption, we may explicitly write the denominator of
\zeta_{\Phi}
as the Dirichlet polynomial

P(s)=1-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{s}=1-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{0}^{k_{\varphi}s}.

Under the change of variables
s=\log_{\lambda_{0}}z
(so that
\lambda_{0}^{s}=z
), we have that

P(\log_{\lambda_{0}}z)=1-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}z^{k_{\varphi}}.

This is precisely a polynomial in the variable
z
(since
|\Phi|=n<\infty
), so by the fundamental theorem of algebra it has finitely many roots (exactly
K=\max(\left\{k_{\varphi}\right\}_{\varphi\in\Phi})
) in
\mathbb{C}
.

Denote these roots by
Z=\left\{z_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{K}
. Any solution of
P(\omega)=0
must then be of the form
\lambda_{0}^{\omega}=z_{j}
for some
z_{j}\in Z
. We note that
0\notin Z
since the polynomial equals one when
z=0
, so there exists a branch of the logarithm for which
\log z_{j}
is well defined for each
z_{j}\in Z
and without loss of generality we may find a single branch defined for each
z_{j}\in Z
and for
\lambda_{0}
since
Z
is finite. The logarithm is multivalued, however, with
\lambda_{0}^{\omega}=e^{\omega\log\lambda_{0}}=e^{\omega\log\lambda_{0}+2\pi im}
for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. So, we will obtain as solutions to
P(\omega)=0
exactly the points
\omega_{j,m}
of the form
\omega_{j,m}=\log(z_{j})/\log(\lambda_{0})+2\pi im/\log(\lambda_{0})
.

Observe that there are finitely many real parts,
\sigma_{j}=\text{Re}(\log(z_{j})/\log(\lambda_{0}))
,
j=1,...,K
, at which these poles occur and that the imaginary parts are all distributed with the same period,
2\pi/\log\lambda_{0}
. We will show that for any screen of the form
S_{\sigma}(\tau)\equiv\sigma
,
\sigma\neq\sigma_{j}
for each
j=1,...,K
,
\zeta_{\Phi}
is languid with exponent
\kappa=0
with respect to
S_{\sigma}
, starting with hypothesis
L1
.

The next two steps of the proof are the same as in the proof of Theorem
2.4.5
. In short, by the strong languidity of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, for any
\sigma_{-}
, there exists a sequence of admissible heights
\left\{T_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
on which
\zeta_{\Phi}
is bounded on horizontal intervals of the form
[\sigma_{-}+iT_{n},\sigma_{+}+iT_{n}]
where
\sigma_{-}
is arbitrarily small and
\sigma_{+}>\max(\dim_{S}(\Phi),\sigma_{-})
. Choosing
\sigma_{-}=\sigma
establishes hypothesis
L1
with respect to the screen
S_{\sigma}
for
\zeta_{\Phi}
. Secondly, we have that
\zeta_{R}
satisfies hypotheses
L1
and
L2
for the sequence of heights as above on any screen of the form
S_{\sigma}
,
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
, because it is a holomorphic Mellin transform.

It remains only to show that
\zeta_{\Phi}
satisfies hypothesis
L2
on
S_{\sigma}
when
\sigma
is not one of the exceptional values
\sigma_{j}
,
j=1,...,K
. We will show that
f(\tau)
is bounded from below by a strictly positive constant on all of
\mathbb{R}
, and thus
\zeta_{\Phi}
will be bounded on
S_{\sigma}
. As we have shown,
P(\sigma+i\tau)\neq 0
whenever
\sigma\notin\left\{\sigma_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{K}
. Let
f(\tau)=|P(\sigma+i\tau)|
. Given any finite interval
[a,b]
, we have that
f(t)
must be nonzero and bounded from below by a strictly positive constant. This follows by continuity and the intermediate value theorem, noting that if
f(\tau)=0
, then
P(\sigma+i\tau)=0
, which is a contradiction.

We will use (multiplicative) periodicity of
f
to show that
f(t)=|P(\sigma+i\tau)|
cannot become arbitrarily small as
|\tau|\to\infty
. Recall that

P(\sigma+i\tau)=1-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\lambda_{0}^{k_{j}(\sigma_{0}+i\tau)}=1-\sum_{j=1}^{K}\lambda_{0}^{k_{j}\sigma}\cdot e^{i(k_{j}\log\lambda_{0})\tau}.

Under the transformation
\tau\mapsto(2\pi m/\log\lambda_{0})\,\tau
, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that each exponential is invariant since
k_{j}
is an integer. Thus,
P(\sigma+i\tau)=P(\sigma+i(2\pi m/\log\lambda_{0})\tau)
. Choosing
m=-1
shows us that the function
f(\tau):=|P(\sigma+i\tau)|
is multiplicatively periodic with period
p=-2\pi/\log\lambda_{0}=2\pi/\log\lambda_{0}^{-1}
or a rational multiple thereof, depending on the greatest common divisor of the integers
k_{j}
,
j=1,...,K
. However, if they share a greatest common divisor
GCD
, it is possible to redefine
\lambda_{0}
by
\lambda_{0}^{\prime}=\lambda_{0}^{\text{GCD}}
and obtain a new set of integers without this property. Note that
\log(\lambda_{0}^{-1})>0
.

By the previous argument,
f(\tau)=|P(\sigma+i\tau)|
is bounded from below on any bounded interval. So,
f
is nonzero on the interval
[-1,1]
. Now pick one full multiplicative period in
(0,\infty)
, say
[p^{m_{0}},p^{m_{0}+1}]
(if
p>1
) or
[p^{m_{0}+1},p^{m_{0}}]
(if
p<1
). The function
f
must be bounded from below strictly away from zero as this is a finite interval. By periodicity, this same bound applies to any interval of the form
[p^{m},p^{m+1}]
or
[p^{m+1},p^{m}]
, respectively, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since the function
p\mapsto p^{t}
is surjective onto
(0,\infty)
, this implies that
f
is bounded uniformly from below by a strictly positive constant, the same as the first bound, when
\tau>0
. For
\tau<0
, we can use the starting interval
[-p^{m_{0}+1},-p^{m_{0}}]
or
[-p^{m_{0}},-p^{m_{0}+1}]
, depending on whether
p>1
or
p<1
, and the same argument to deduce that it is also bounded from below by a strictly positive constant on
(-\infty,0)
. Taking the minimum of these three bounds shows that
f(\tau)=|P(\sigma+i\tau)|>C>0
.

It follows that
\zeta_{\Phi}
is bounded from above on
S_{\sigma_{0}}
, establishing hypothesis
L2
. Thus, we have established the joint languidity of
\zeta_{\Phi}
and
\zeta_{R}
on any screen of the form
S_{\sigma}
, where
\sigma>\sigma_{0}
and where
\sigma\notin\left\{\sigma_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{K}
. When choosing a screen
S_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}
, choosing
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon<\min_{j=1,...,K}(|\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{0}|)
is sufficient.

2.4.3
Zeta Functions from SFEs

Now, we endeavor to solve scaling functional equations in a general framework. The main tool is the truncated Mellin transform (see Section
2.1
),
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}
, where
\delta>0
is fixed. In what follows, we make the convention that
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
is the zeta function
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)
of
f
. The first step of the solution is to apply a fixed Mellin transform,
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}
, to both sides of the SFE. In doing so, we will be able to produce explicit formulae for the zeta function of a solution, prove that it is meromorphic, and most notably to describe explicitly the locations of its possible poles.

The following functions will appear in our analysis, thus we introduce notation for them. Let
L:=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}M_{\lambda_{k}}
be a scaling operator. We define

\xi_{L,f}(s;\delta):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{k}}[f](s)

(2.4.3)

and if
\Phi
is a self-similar system, then define
\xi_{\Phi,f}:=\xi_{L_{\Phi},f}
, where
L_{\Phi}
is the scaling operator associated to
\Phi
. Additionally, given the functions
f
and
R
, we define
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)
. Lastly, we recall that
\zeta_{L}
is the scaling zeta function of
L
, with singular set
\mathcal{D}_{L}
(see Definition
2.2.2
).

Theorem 2.4.11

(Zeta Functions of Solutions to General SFEs)
.

Let
f,R\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
and suppose that there exist
\sigma_{R},\sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
and
f(t)=O(t^{\sigma_{1}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Suppose that
L:=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}M_{\lambda_{k}}
is a scaling operator for which
f
satisfies the scaling functional equation
f=L[f]+R
for all
t\in(0,\delta]
.

Then for all
s\in\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{L}
,

\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{L}(s)(\xi_{L,f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)),

(2.4.4)

where
\xi_{L,f}
, defined as in Equation
2.4.3
, is an entire function. Further,
\zeta_{f}
is holomorphic in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\max(D,\sigma_{R})}
, where
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
, and admits a meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
with any poles contained in
\mathcal{D}_{L}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}})
. Further, these poles are independent of the choice of
\delta>0
.

Proof 2.4.12
.

We note that by Lemma
2.1.2
,
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)
are well-defined and holomorphic in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\max(\sigma_{R},\sigma_{1})}
. Additionally, we have that by Lemma
2.1.6
, for each
M_{\lambda_{k}}
,

\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[M_{\lambda_{k}}[f]](s)=\lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{M}^{\delta/\lambda_{k}}[f](s),

noting that
M_{\lambda_{k}}
is defined with a reciprocal scaling convention. It follows that
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[M_{\lambda_{k}}[f]]
is well-defined and holomorphic in this same half-plane for each
k=1,...,m
.

So, we may apply the truncated scaling transform
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}
to each term of the scaling functional equation
f=L[f]+R
, using linearity of the Mellin transform to commute with the summation. Thus, we compute that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}M_{\lambda_{k}}[f]\right](s)+\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[M_{\lambda_{k}}[f]](s)+\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{M}^{\delta/\lambda_{k}}[f](s)+\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)+\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{s}\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{k}}[f](s)+\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s).

In the last step, we have partitioned the integrals defining each Mellin transform, splitting each integral over
[0,\delta/\lambda_{k}]
into two integrals, over
[0,\delta]
and
(\delta,\delta/\lambda_{k}]
respectively. Also, we note that the second sum is exactly the definition of
\xi_{L,f}
. Rearranging this equation and using the definitions of
\zeta_{f}
and
\zeta_{R}
, we find that

\displaystyle\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)\Big(1-\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\lambda_{k}^{s}\Big)=\xi_{L,f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta).

Provided that
s\in\mathbb{H}_{\max(\sigma_{R},\sigma_{1})}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{L}
, it follows that
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)=\zeta_{L}(s)(\xi_{L,f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))
.

From this equation, we can deduce some properties of
\zeta_{f}
. Firstly, by Lemma
2.1.2
, we have that each function
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{k}}[f]
,
k=1,...,m
, is entire (using the fact that since
f
is continuous, it is bounded on any compact interval of the form
[\alpha,\beta]
). Since
\xi_{L,f}
is a finite linear combination of these functions, each multiplied by the respective entire function
\lambda_{k}^{s}
, it must also be entire. Secondly, we have that the right-hand side is holomorphic in the half plane
\mathbb{H}_{\max(D,\sigma_{R})}
. Thus,
\zeta_{f}
admits an analytic continuation to this region and is holomorphic therein (irrespective of the value of
\sigma_{1}
). Additionally, since
\zeta_{L}
admits a meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane, we find that
\zeta_{f}
admits a meromorphic continuation to the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
where
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic, thus having poles with a given multiplicity at a point only if the function
\zeta_{L}
has a pole of that multiplicity of higher at that point.

Lastly, the independence of the poles of
\zeta_{f}
on the choice of
\delta
may be seen as a corollary of Lemma
2.1.2
. For any
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
, we have that
\mathcal{M}^{\delta_{2}}[f](s)-\mathcal{M}^{\delta_{1}}[f](s)=\mathcal{M}_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}[f](s)
, which is an entire function. Thus, these two functions must have the same poles.

We now specialize to operators of the form
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
, where
\Phi
is a self-similar system and
\alpha>0
. We require that
\alpha>0
so that
\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}\in(0,1)
for each
\varphi\in\Phi
. When
\alpha=1
, this is exactly the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}
associated to
\Phi
. For other values of
\alpha
, the effect on the associated zeta function is simply a rescaling of the input relative to
\zeta_{\Phi}
, namely
\zeta_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}(s)=\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
. (This is an immediate consequence of the definition of these functions and elementary properties of exponents.) Note that it follows that if
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
is the abscissa of convergence of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, then
\zeta_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}
has the corresponding abscissa of convergence
D/\alpha
. Further, the sets of poles are directly related: any pole
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
exactly corresponds to the pole
\omega/\alpha\in\mathcal{D}_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}
.

These observations together yield the following corollary of Theorem
2.4.11
.

Corollary 2.4.13

(Zeta Functions of Solutions to Self-Similar SFEs)
.

Let
f,R\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
and suppose that there exist
\sigma_{R},\sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
and
f(t)=O(t^{\sigma_{1}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
\alpha>0
, and let
{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
. If
f
satisfies the scaling functional equation
f={L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}[f]+R
for all
t\in(0,\delta]
, then

\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))

(2.4.5)

and is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})}
, where
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
. Further,
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)
is meromorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
with poles contained in a subset of
\alpha^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}})
and are independent of
\delta
.

2.4.4
Mellin Inversion

The next step of solving a scaling functional equation is to apply the Mellin inversion theorem. This result is analogous to the Fourier inversion theorem, and provides a means by which one may invert the Mellin transform; see for instance
[
Tit86
]
. We shall make use of the following notation convention:

\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}f(z)\,dz:=\lim_{T\to\infty}\int_{c-iT}^{c+iT}f(z)\,dz,

which may also be considered a principle value integral such as in
[
Gra10
]
. Combined with Theorem
2.4.11
, the function may be written in terms of
\zeta_{\Phi}
,
\zeta_{R}
, and
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
.

Theorem 2.4.14

(Mellin Inversion Formula)
.

Let
f,R\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
\alpha>0
, and let
{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
. Suppose that
f
satisfies the scaling functional equation
f={L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}}[f]+R
for all
t\in[0,\delta]
and that
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[R](s)
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
. Lastly, let
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
and
c>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
. Then for any
t\in(0,\delta)
,
f
is given by:

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}^{-1}[\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))](t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}t^{-s}\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))\,ds.

Proof 2.4.15
.

This result is an application of the standard Mellin inversion theorem applied to the result of Corollary
2.4.13
. Technically, we modify the function
f
slightly, namely by considering
f(t)\mathds{1}_{[0,\delta]}(t)
which is the same as
f
when
t\leq\delta
. By Corollary
2.4.13
, we have that
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
is holomorphic when
\text{Re}(\alpha s)=\alpha\text{Re}(s)>D
and thus that
\mathcal{M}[f\cdot\mathds{1}_{[0,\delta]}]=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f]
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})}
.

Thus by application of the Mellin inversion theorem to Equation
2.4.5
, we have that for any
c>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
,

f(t)\cdot\mathds{1}_{[0,\delta]}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}t^{-s}\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))\,ds.

Since
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that
\mathds{1}_{[0,\delta]}(t)=1
, whence the result follows.

It remains to evaluate this contour integral by means of the residue theorem. Strictly speaking, it requires a sort of unbounded residue theorem, using a sequence of regions for which the integrand has finitely many poles (on which the residue theorem applies) that converge to the region containing all of the countably many singularities. The conditions of languidity are necessary in this process, used to estimate contour integrals over pieces of the boundaries of regions involved in this process. This method developed to establish explicit formulae for fractal tube functions in
[
LvF13
]
and then, with refinements to the proof, used to prove explicit formulae for relative tube functions in
[
LRŽ17
]
. Applying this method in the general setting to obtain explicit formulae is the content Section
2.5
.

2.5
Solutions of Scaling Functional Equations

In this section, we will obtain explicit formulae for functions which satisfy a scaling functional equation. Given a scaling operator
L
, a scaling functional equation is a relation of the form
f=L[f]+R
, where
R
is a remainder term. The goal will be to obtain formulae which are valid up to an asymptotic order determined by estimates of an
admissible remainder
term (in the sense of Definition
2.4.4
).

We focus on scaling functional equations which are induced by a self-similar system on functions which satisfy certain scaling laws (see Proposition
2.4.3
), as these are the main type of scaling functional equation that we will need for our applications in Chapters
3
and
4
. Explicitly, let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
\alpha>0
, and define the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}
. The scaling functional equations we study herein will be stated in terms of such scaling operators. Note, though, that any general scaling operator with scaling ratios in
(0,1)
each having positive, integral multiplicity can be written in this form.

There are two types of explicit formulae, namely pointwise and distributional. The former have the advantage of being simpler in their statements, but the disadvantage that they are derived only for antiderivatives of the function satisfying the scaling functional equation. The distributional explicit formulae we obtain do not have this restriction, but they will require additional terminology to state and interpret in a weak formulation.

2.5.1
Pointwise Explicit Formulae

The pointwise explicit formulae are for antiderivatives of a function
f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
which satisfies a scaling functional equation. To that end, we introduce the following notation and convention. Firstly, define
f^{[0]}:=f
. For any
k>0
, we define
f^{[k]}
recursively by integrating the previous antiderivative and imposing the convention that
f^{[k]}(0)=0
. Namely, for
k>0
,

f^{[k]}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}f^{[k-1]}(\tau)\,d\tau.

We will also denote
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
. In our applications, typically we have some function
F(t)
of interest which must be normalized in order to satisfy a scaling law in the sense of Definition
2.4.2
, so we will have that
f(t)=t^{-\beta}F(t)
for a given parameter
\beta
. For tube functions in Chapter
3
,
\beta
will be the dimension
N
and for heat content in Chapter
4
it will be
N/2
. When we state the explicit formula, we will do so in terms of the parameter
\beta
, noting that typically
\beta=N
. Let
F^{[k]}
be defined in the same manner as
f^{[k]}
,
k\geq 0
.

As an additional preliminary to stating the result, we define the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{w}:=\Gamma(z+w)/\Gamma(w)
for
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that when
w=k
is a positive integer, this simplifies to
(z)_{k}=z(z+1)\cdots(z+k-1)
or, when
w=0
, to
(z)_{0}=1
.

Lastly, we must give meaning to the summations which will appear in what follows. Let
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{C}
be a discrete subset with the property that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{D}_{m}:=\left\{\omega\in\mathcal{D}\,\middle|\,|\text{Im}(\omega)|\leq m\right\}<\infty.

Then we define

\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}}a_{\omega}:=\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{m}}a_{\omega}.

Note that this summation is a
symmetric limit
of finite partial sums. As such, the convergence of these sums is comparatively more delicate; it may be that the limit, when taken independently, does not exist.

Theorem 2.5.1

(Pointwise Explicit Formula)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system,
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
f(t)=t^{-\beta/\alpha}F(t)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Suppose that
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
f=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f]+R
on
[0,\delta]
with admissible remainder term
R
(in the sense of Definition
2.4.4
) with corresponding screen
S
. Let
\sigma_{R}
denote the abscissa of holomorphicity of
\zeta_{R}
,
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
the (upper) similarity dimension of
\Phi
, and suppose
\beta/\alpha+1>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
. Lastly, suppose that
S
is contained in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
and write
W_{S}
for the window to the right of
S
.

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that

F^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{(\beta-s)/\alpha+k}}{((\beta-s)/\alpha+1)_{k}}\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
\zeta_{f}
is given by Equation
2.4.5
. The error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{\beta/\alpha-\sup(S)+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Note, in particular, that the order of the remainder directly controls the error of the approximation. As we are considering small values of
t
, the larger the exponent, the better the remainder estimate. The restriction of
k\geq 2
is related to the exponent
\kappa=0
of the languid growth of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, and consequently of
\zeta_{f}
.

The leading order term occurs at the pole
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
, as this is the abscissa of convergence of
\zeta_{f}
. If
D
is a simple pole, the only pole with real part
D
, and supposing
D>\sigma_{R}
, then the leading order term is explicitly

\frac{1}{((\beta-D)/\alpha+1)_{k}}\Res(\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha;\delta);D)\,t^{(\beta-D)/\alpha+k}.

This occurs when the scaling ratios
\left\{\lambda_{\varphi}\right\}_{\varphi\in\Phi}
are non-arithmetically related, called the nonlattice case (see, for example, Definition 2.13 in
[
LvF13
]
).

2.5.2
Distributional Explicit Formulae

Put simply, a
distribution
is a functional on a prescribed space of functions called
test functions
. A standard distribution is a functional on the space
C_{c}^{\infty}
of smooth, compactly supported functions on a given domain. The type of distributions we consider here, called
tempered distributions
will be a subspace of these distributions.

We define as the space of test functions the class of Schwartz functions, or functions of rapid decrease. For a finite domain
(0,\delta)
, just as in Chapter 5 of
[
LvF13
]
and Chapter 5 of
[
LRŽ17
]
, the space is defined by

\mathcal{S}(0,\delta):=\left\{\psi\in C^{\infty}(0,\delta)\,\middle|\,\begin{aligned} &\forall m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\forall q\in\mathbb{N},\text{ as }t\to 0^{+},\\
&t^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\text{ and }(t-\delta)^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\,\end{aligned}\right\}.

(2.5.1)

Note that
C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\delta)\subseteq\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
, with a continuous embedding, whence by duality the space of functionals on
\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
is a subset of the usual space of distributions, the dual of
C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\delta)
. Typically, for a distribution
F
and a test function
\psi
, we write
F(\psi)=\langle F,\psi\rangle
, denoting by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the natural pairing of
\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
with its dual space.

In this distributional setting, the restriction of
k\geq 2
may be relaxed in Theorem
2.5.1
. However, equality must be interpreted
in the sense of distributions
: we say that
F=G
in the sense of distributions if for any test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,
\langle F,\psi\rangle=\langle G,\psi\rangle
. In addition to the standard definitions for the definitions of differentiation and integration of distributions, we note the following relevant identity regarding residues at a point:

\langle\Res(t^{\beta}G(s);\omega),\psi\rangle=\Res(\mathcal{M}[\psi](\beta+1)G(s);\omega).

(2.5.2)

See Equation 5.2.5 in
[
LRŽ17
]
, as well as the preceding discussion regarding the extension of
\psi
to all of
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

Secondly, we provide the definition for a distribution
\mathcal{R}
to satisfy the error estimate
O(t^{\alpha})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Given a test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
and
a>0
, define the new test function
\psi_{a}(t):=a^{-1}\psi(t/a)
. We say that
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{\alpha})
as
t\to 0^{+}
if for all
a>0
and for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\langle\mathcal{R},\psi_{a}(t)\rangle=O(a^{\alpha}),

(2.5.3)

as
t\to 0^{+}
in the usual sense.

Theorem 2.5.2

(Distributional Explicit Formula)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system,
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
f(t)=t^{-\beta/\alpha}F(t)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Suppose that
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
f=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f]+R
on
[0,\delta]
with admissible remainder term
R
(in the sense of Definition
2.4.4
) with corresponding screen
S
. Let
\sigma_{R}
denote the abscissa of holomorphicity of
\zeta_{R}
,
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
the (upper) similarity dimension of
\Phi
, and suppose
\beta/\alpha+1>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
. Lastly, suppose that
S
is contained in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
and write
W_{S}
for the window to the right of
S
.

Then for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that, in the sense of distributions,
F^{[k]}
satisfies

\begin{split}F^{[k]}(t)&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{(\beta-s)/\alpha+k}}{((\beta-s)/\alpha+1)_{k}}\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{[k]}(t),\end{split}

(2.5.4)

as
t\to 0^{+}
. See Equation
2.5.5
for the explicit identity of action on test functions.

Here,
\zeta_{f}
is as in Corollary
2.4.13
. The distributional remainder term satisfies the estimate
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{\beta/\alpha-\sup(S)+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in the sense of Equation
2.5.3
.

Most precisely, Equation
2.5.4
means that for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\begin{split}\langle F^{[k]},\psi\rangle&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{\mathcal{M}[\psi]({(\beta-s)/\alpha+k+1})}{((\beta-s)/\alpha+1)_{k}}\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha);\omega\Bigg)+\langle\mathcal{R}^{[k]},\psi\rangle.\end{split}

(2.5.5)

While this formulation is less direct compared to the pointwise expansion, it does have the advantage of requiring less regularity to leverage the expansion. Namely, it is valid when
k=0
, yielding a formula (in the sense of distributions) for the function
f
itself.

Corollary 2.5.3

(Distributional Explicit Formula,
k=0
)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system,
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
f(t)=t^{-\beta/\alpha}F(t)\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Suppose that
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
f=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f]+R
on
[0,\delta]
with admissible remainder term
R
(in the sense of Definition
2.4.4
) with corresponding screen
S
. Let
\sigma_{R}
denote the abscissa of holomorphicity of
\zeta_{R}
,
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
the (upper) similarity dimension of
\Phi
, and suppose
\beta/\alpha+1>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
. Lastly, suppose that
S
is contained in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
and write
W_{S}
for the window to the right of
S
.

Then in the sense of distributions we have that for
t\in[0,\delta)
,

F(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})}\Res\big(t^{{\beta-s}/\alpha}\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha;\delta);\omega\big)+\mathcal{R}(t).

(2.5.6)

The distributional remainder term satisfies the estimate
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{\beta/\alpha-\sup(S)})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in the sense of Equation
2.5.3
.

If we further assume that the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
in
W_{S}
are simple, then Equation
2.5.6
simplifies to

f(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})}\Res(\zeta_{f}(s/\alpha;\delta);\omega)\,t^{(\beta-\omega)/\alpha}+\mathcal{R}(t).

In this formulation, and in particular in the case when the self-similar system induces simple poles of its zeta function, the formula is a simple expansion with constant coefficients and powers determined by the poles
\omega
. The computation of the residues, however, is not a trivial matter and beyond the current scope of this work. Corollary
2.5.3
is a direct application of Theorem
2.5.2
.

2.5.3
Languidity from Scaling Functional Equations

Before we can prove the explicit formulae, we shall have need of estimating
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
. In particular, using the formula in Corollary
2.4.13
, we show that
\zeta_{f}
is languid provided some control over the remainder term itself.

Theorem 2.5.4

(Languidity of Zeta Functions from SFEs)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
\alpha>0
, and
f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{+})
. Suppose that
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
f=L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}[f]+R
on
[0,\delta]
with admissible remainder term
R
for
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
(in the sense of Definition
2.4.4
) and let
S
be an admissible screen.

Then the zeta function
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[f](s)
is also languid with exponent
\kappa=0
with respect to
S
.

Proof 2.5.5
.

Because
R
is an admissible remainder for
L_{\Phi}^{\alpha}
with admissible screen
S
, we have that
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)
are jointly languid (with exponent
\kappa=0
) with respect to this screen
S
. We have that
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
is an entire function by Corollary
2.4.13
and explicitly we show that
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
is bounded on any vertical strip of the form
\mathbb{H}_{a}^{b}=\left\{s\in\mathbb{C}\,\middle|\,a<\text{Re}(s)<b\right\}
.

In order to do so, it suffices to find constants
C_{\xi},A_{\xi}
such that
|\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)|\leq C_{\xi}A_{\xi}^{|\text{Re}(s)|}
, as this function is bounded when
|\text{Re}(s)|
is bounded as is the case with vertical strips.

To that end, let
\sigma=\text{Re}(s)
and define
\Lambda_{+}=\max(\left\{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}\right\}_{\varphi\in\Phi})
and
\Lambda_{-}=\min(\left\{\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}\right\}_{\varphi\in\Phi})
. Then we have that

\displaystyle|\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha\sigma}\left|\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}[f](s)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\max(\Lambda_{+}^{\sigma},\Lambda_{-}^{\sigma})\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\left|\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}[f](s)\right|.

By Corollary
2.1.4
, we have that each Mellin transform
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha}}[f]
is bounded on any vertical strip of the form
\mathbb{H}_{a}^{b}
. It follows that a finite sum of such functions is bounded on any such vertical strip by the sum of these constants, which we denote by
C_{\xi}
. Choosing
A_{\xi}=\max(\Lambda_{-},\Lambda_{+},\Lambda_{-}^{-1},\Lambda_{+}^{-1})
allows us to write that
A_{\xi}^{|\sigma|}\geq\max(\Lambda_{+}^{\sigma},\Lambda_{-}^{\sigma})
, and thus obtaining the desired bound. It follows that
|\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}|
is is bounded in any vertical strip.

Now let
\left\{\tau_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of admissible heights shared jointly by
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)
, which exists by fiat. We have that each of these respective functions is uniformly bounded on all of the horizontal contours
I_{n}=[S(\tau)+i\tau_{n},c+i\tau_{n}]
, for some particular
c
sufficiently large so that
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
and
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)
are holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{c}
. Since
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
is bounded on the strip
\mathbb{H}_{\inf(S)}^{c}
, it follows that it is uniformly bounded on all of these intervals
I_{n}\subset\mathbb{H}_{\inf(S)}^{c}
. It follows that
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{\Phi,f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))
is uniformly bounded on these intervals. This establishes hypothesis
L1
for the screen
S
.

For hypothesis
L2
, we note that
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)
and
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
are bounded on
S
by assumption of their joint languidity. Because
S
is contained in the strip
\mathbb{H}_{\inf(S)}^{\sup(S)}
(noting that screens are by definition bounded), we have that
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
is bounded on
S
. The boundedness of
\zeta_{f}
on this screen thus follows. Thus,
\zeta_{f}
is languid with respect to the same screen
S
(with exponent
\kappa
).

2.5.4
Proof of the Explicit Formulae

With the languid growth conditions of
\zeta_{f}
having been established, we may now complete the proofs of the main theorems, Theorems
2.5.1
and
2.5.2
. We shall treat the proofs of these theorems together as they require similar setup and estimates. The main differences come in the application of different theorems to provide the relevant result. Starting from the result of Theorem
2.4.14
, we will compute the resulting contour integral by the methods in Theorem 5.1.11 and Theorem 5.2.2 of
[
LRŽ17
]
for the pointwise and distributional explicit formula, respectively.

Proof 2.5.6
.

Fix
c
such that
\beta/\alpha+1>c>\max(D/\alpha,\sigma_{R})
. By Theorem
2.4.14
, we have that for any
t\in(0,\delta)
,

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}t^{-s}\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))\,ds,

where
\zeta_{f}
,
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
, and
\zeta_{R}
are as in Corollary
2.4.13
. By Theorem
2.5.4
, we have that
\zeta_{f}
is languid with exponent
\kappa=0
on the admissible screen
S
.

Noting that
F(t)=t^{\beta/\alpha}f(t)
, we have that

\displaystyle F(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}t^{\beta/\alpha-s}\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)(\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta))\,ds.

It is this expression that is analogous to Equation 5.1.18 in
[
LRŽ17
]
, but with
\beta
in place of
N
,
\alpha=1
, and
\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)
in place of the relative tube zeta function
\widetilde{\zeta}_{A,\Omega}(s;\delta)
. Under these conditions, Theorems 5.1.11 and Theorem 5.2.2 of
[
LRŽ17
]
are essentially applicable with only minor modifications and justifications required in the general case.

Firstly, Proposition 5.1.8 of
[
LRŽ17
]
is applicable, where the interchange of the order of integration may justified by the explicit formula for
\zeta_{f}
(c.f. Corollary
2.4.13
) and explicit bounds for the constituent functions. The uniform estimates for
\zeta_{R}
and
\xi_{L_{\Phi}^{\alpha},f}
follow from Corollary
2.1.4
.

For an estimate of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, note that when
\sigma=\text{Re}(s)>D/\alpha
,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha s}\right|\leq p(\alpha\sigma):=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{\alpha\sigma}<p(D)=1,

using the technique in Proposition
2.2.5
(viz.
p
is strictly decreasing). It follows that there is a uniform bound (based on
c>D/\alpha
) for
\zeta_{\Phi}(\alpha s)
when
\text{Re}(s)=c
. Put together, we obtain that the function
t^{\beta/\alpha-s}\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)
is integrable for
(t,s)\in[0,\delta]\times(c-i\infty,c+i\infty)
provided that
\beta/\alpha+1>c>D/\alpha
. The assumption that
\beta/\alpha+1>c
ensures that
t^{\beta/\alpha-s}
is integrable as
t\to 0^{+}
. Next, Lemma 5.1.10 (with
\kappa=0
) is directly applicable based on the languidity of the function
\zeta_{f}
(see Theorem
2.5.4
) followed by Theorem 5.1.11 of
[
LRŽ17
]
. This establishes proving Theorem
2.5.1
.

This integrability argument also is required to justify the use of the Fubini–Tonelli theorem in Equation 5.2.10 (for the proof of Theorem 5.5.5) in
[
LRŽ17
]
. In this case, because
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
, we may write the corresponding integral in question as

\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\psi(t)\frac{t^{\beta/\alpha-s+k}}{(\beta/\alpha-s+1)_{k}}\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)\,dt=\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\int_{0}^{\delta}\psi(t)\frac{t^{\beta/\alpha-s+k}}{(\beta/\alpha-s+1)_{k}}\zeta_{f}(s;\delta)\,dt.

We claim that once again, the integrand is integrable over the product space, that is for
(t,s)\in[0,\delta]\times(c-i\infty,c+i\infty)
. Firstly, we have the same estimates for
\zeta_{f}
as before. For the Pochhammer symbol, we may write that

\displaystyle|(\beta/\alpha-s+1)_{k}|

\displaystyle\geq|\beta/\alpha-c+1|\cdot|\beta/\alpha-c+2|\cdots|\beta/\alpha-c+k-1|

\displaystyle\geq|\beta/\alpha-c+1|^{k},

noting that on the vertical lines
z=\beta/\alpha-c+k+i\mathbb{R}
, the closest point to the origin occurs exactly on the real axis. The second estimate follows from the fact that
\beta/\alpha-c+1>0
is the smallest term in the product. This gives a uniform upper bound for
|(\beta/\alpha-s+1)_{k}^{-1}|
. Lastly, the power of
t
may be arbitrary since
\psi
is a function of rapid decrease: for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
t^{\gamma}\psi(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

The final step is a minor change of variables. Rather than indexing the sum by the poles of
\zeta_{f}
in
W_{S}
, we note that as long as
\text{Re}(s)>\sigma_{R}
, the abscissa of holomorphicity of
\zeta_{R}
, a pole of
\zeta_{f}
only occurs at
s
if
\omega=\alpha s
is a pole of
\zeta_{\Phi}
. In other words, we have that

\sum_{s\in\mathcal{D}_{f}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}})}g(s)=\sum_{\omega/\alpha\in\mathcal{D}_{f}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}})}g(\omega/\alpha)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\alpha\sigma_{R}})}g(\omega/\alpha).

Since we assume that
S
lies in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
, this same argument applies with respect to the window
W_{S}
and its scaled transformation
\alpha W_{S}
.

Chapter 3
Geometry of Fractals

In this chapter, we apply our results in the general framework to establish explicit formulae for
relative tube functions
. These functions describe the volume of a tubular neighborhood, obtained by thickening up a given set, as a function of the distance parameter describing the thickness. Strictly speaking, we concern ourselves with relative neighborhoods: after thickening up the set, we then intersect with some other set to which the neighborhood is considered relative. Given a
relative fractal drum

(X,\Omega)
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the relative tube function is
|X_{t}\cap\Omega|_{N}
, where
|\cdot|_{N}
is the
N
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

A key notion in the study of fractals is that of
complex dimensions
. They may be defined explicitly starting from these tube functions (which produce tube zeta functions), our main object of study in this chapter. As we will see, the complex dimensions play a pivotal role in producing explicit formulae for the tube functions. We show that the possible complex dimensions of a self-similar set
X
, the invariant set of a self-similar system
\Phi
, are controlled exactly by the poles of the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
. This requires some appropriate separation conditions, including the open set condition.

The results in this work determine the possible complex dimensions for a wide variety of self-similar fractals in dimensions two and larger. Most notably, our method allows the study of self-similar fractals which are not made up of a union of disjoint sets. Instead, the tubular neighborhoods may partition into scaled pieces only up to a given remainder term. We show that this induces a scaling functional equation for the normalized tube function, leading to the application of our results from Chapter
2
. We conclude with the explicit computation of the tube functions and possible complex dimensions for generalized von Koch fractals.

3.1
Relative Tube Functions

In what follows, let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. We recall that per Definition
1.2.2
, this is a tuple of sets
X,\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the condition that
\Omega
is open, has finite Lebesgue measure, and that there exists some
\delta>0
so that

\Omega_{\delta}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\,\middle|\,\exists\,y\in\Omega,\,|x-y|<\delta\right\}\supseteq X.

Let
|\cdot|_{N}
denote the
N
-dimensional Lebesgue measure. A set of the form
\Omega_{\delta}
is called a
tubular neighborhood
of
\Omega
. If we consider a tubular neighborhood
X_{t}
of the set
X
, a
relative tubular neighborhood
is the set
X_{t}\cap\Omega
. We will concern ourselves with the following problem: given a relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
, find a formula for the volume of the relative tubular neighborhood
X_{t}\cap\Omega
. Note that we are using volume as a general term for
N
-dimensional measure; it is understood to be area in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and length in
\mathbb{R}
.

3.1.1
Tubular Neighborhoods and Tube Functions

Given a set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
t\geq 0
, we say that a
tubular neighborhood
of
X
is any set of the form

X_{t}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\exists\,x\in X,\,|y-x|<t\}.

Typically, we care about these sets for small, positive values of
t=\varepsilon>0
. For this reason, tubular neighborhoods may also be called
\varepsilon
-neighborhoods, especially when one wishes to explicitly specify the value of the parameter. If
t=0
, then a
0
-neighborhood is simply the set itself. If the set
X
is bounded, then it lies within some ball of radius
R
about the origin. For large
t\gg R
, the
t
-neighborhood of
X
will asymptotically behave simply like the neighborhood of this ball
B_{R}(0)
. So, the most interesting behavior occurs for small values of
t>0
. Generally speaking, we will fix
\delta>0
and let
t\in(0,\delta)
, considering the limit as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Note that even if
X
itself is not Lebesgue measurable, its tubular neighborhoods
X_{t}
for any
t>0
are automatically measurable. Note that the following is an equivalent definition for a tubular neighborhood:

X_{t}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:\exists\,x\in X,\,|y-x|<t\}=\bigcup_{x\in X}B_{t}(x).

The latter set is an arbitrary union of open sets
B_{t}(x)
, the open ball of radius
t
centered at
X
, and thus is also open. By the construction of Lebesgue measure, open sets are all measurable.

Given a set
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the function

V_{X}(t):=\left|X_{t}\right|_{N},

defined for any
t>0
, is called the
tube function
of
X
. Note that by the properties of Lebesgue measure, it follows that
V_{X}
is continuous on
(0,\infty)
and decreasing. If
X
is bounded, we have that
V_{X}(t)
is finite and strictly decreasing for any
t>0
. If
X
is measurable, then
V_{X}(0)
is well-defined.

3.1.2
Tube Formulae and Codimension

A formula for the tube function of a set
X
is naturally connected to the dimensions of the set
X
and its constituent pieces. Consider for a moment some simple shapes with clear dimension: a point
P=\left\{(0,0)\right\}
, a unit line segment
L=[0,1]
, a unit square
S=[0,1]\times[0,1]
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Figure
3.1
depicts the tubular neighborhoods of these shapes, which can be computed explicitly:

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{P}(t)&=\pi t^{2-0},\\
V_{L}(t)&=\pi t^{2-0}+2t^{2-1},\\
V_{S}(t)&=\pi t^{2-0}+4t^{2-1}+t^{2-2}.\end{split}

(3.1.1)

These formulae have been written in a particular fashion: each term is of the form
t^{N-k}
, where
k
is the dimension corresponding to a piece of the shape contributing to the neighborhood and
N=2
. The point has a neighborhood from only a
0
-dimensional piece. The line segment has contributions from its endpoints (forming a full circle), and contributions from the segment itself. The square has contributions from its corners, from its edges, and from the area of the square itself. In other words, the exponents of the distance parameter
t
in the tube formulae are the
codimensions
of components of the set.

Figure 3.1:
Tubular neighborhoods of a point, a line segment, and a unit square in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The volume of these neighborhoods, given by the tube formulae in Equation
3.1.1
, contain contributions of the form
t^{2-k}
corresponding to pieces of the neighborhood arising from
k
-dimensional component of the set. Partition lines within the neighborhood have been added to delineate these pieces.

Now instead consider a fractal
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. If we obtain a tube formula for
\left|X_{t}\right|_{N}
, it may contain terms of the form
t^{N-D}
, where
D\in[0,N]
need not be an integer. In fact, if the formula contains periodic terms—corresponding to
geometric oscillations
— we can take the Fourier expansion of these periodic terms and the formula can contain terms of the form
t^{N-\omega}
where
\omega
is a
complex
number. Writing
\omega=\sigma+i\tau
, a single term can be rewritten as

t^{N-\omega}=t^{N-\sigma}e^{-i\tau\log t}=t^{N-\sigma}(\cos(\tau\log t)-i\sin(\tau\log t)).

The real part of
\omega
controls the amplitude of this term and the imaginary part controls the multiplicative period. For the latter, note that when
t^{\prime}=e^{2\pi/\tau}t
,
\tau\log t^{\prime}=\tau\log t+2\pi
which is a shift by the period of the sine and cosine.

It is through this means that we can make sense of the
complex dimensions
of fractals. It also explains why a set will typically have multiple complex dimensions. We will take the approach of defining fractal zeta functions through
relative tube zeta functions
which rely on relative tube functions. However, the complex dimensions of a set may be obtained by different types of zeta functions, yielding the exact same set of complex dimensions. For instance, a
distance zeta function
has the same poles as a tube zeta function; see Corollary 2.2.10 in
[
LRŽ17
]
.

3.1.3
Relative Tube Functions

Our main object of study is a relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Before computing the volume of a tubular neighborhood of
X
, we will first intersect with the relative set
\Omega
. The resulting set
X_{t}\cap\Omega
is called a
relative tubular neighborhood
of
X
. The condition that
\Omega
is measurable with finite Lebesgue measure (see Definition
1.2.2
) implies that for any
t>0
,
X_{t}\cap\Omega
is measurable and that
\left|X_{t}\cap\Omega\right|_{N}<\infty
.

Note that this allows for
X
to be an unbounded set, so long as there is a finite set
\Omega
such that some tubular neighborhood of
\Omega
contains
X
. For example, take
X
to be Gabriel’s horn, the surface of revolution in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
formed by rotating the curve
y=1/x
,
x\in(0,\infty)
, about the
x
-axis. It is well-known that
X
has infinite surface area but finite volume. Taking
\Omega
to be the open region bounded by
X
and the plane
x=0
,
(X,\Omega)
is a relative (fractal) drum. Note that
X\subset\partial\Omega
, so
X
is contained in any tubular neighborhood of
\Omega
.

The tube function of
(X,\Omega)
shall be the volume of a relative tubular neighborhood of
X
as a function of the distance parameter. For this reason, we call them
relative
tube functions to distinguish from the tube function of
X
itself. We note that the definition works even if
(X,\Omega)
is not a relative fractal drum provided that
X_{t}\cap\Omega
is measurable with finite measure.

Definition 3.1.1

(Relative Tube Function)
.

Let
X,\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and suppose that
\Omega
is a Lebesgue measurable set. Suppose either that
\Omega
is of finite measure or that
X
is bounded. The
relative tube function

V_{X,\Omega}
is the function defined by
V_{X,\Omega}(t)=\left|X_{t}\cap\Omega\right|_{N}
for any
t>0
and at
t=0
if
X
is measurable.

We say that
V_{X,\Omega}
is the tube function of
X
relative to
\Omega
, and if
(X,\Omega)
is a relative fractal drum, then it is the tube function of
(X,\Omega)
. In a relative fractal drum,
\Omega
is assumed to be open (hence measurable) and having finite Lebesgue measure, so this function is well-defined on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. We do not need to assume that
X
is measurable (as for
t>0
, its tubular neighborhoods are open). Note that if we choose
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then we obtain a standard tube function for
X
, which is finite when
X
is bounded.

3.1.4
Scaling Properties

The Lebesgue measure has the scaling property that, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\left|\lambda X\right|_{N}=\lambda^{N}\left|X\right|_{N}
. When a tubular neighborhood
X_{t}
is scaled, the parameter
t
defining the new scaled neighborhood will scale linearly. In other words,
\lambda\cdot X_{t}=(\lambda X)_{\lambda t}
. Combining these properties, we may deduce how ordinary tube functions change under scaling: for any
\lambda>0
,
V_{\lambda X}(\lambda t)=\lambda^{N}V_{X}(t)
.

In general, we wish to consider the behavior of a set under the image of a similitude
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
. But such maps are exactly compositions of isometries, which preserve the measure, and uniform scaling maps of the form
x\mapsto\lambda x
. When considering a relative fractal drum, we will also be scaling the corresponding relative set
\Omega
.

Lemma 3.1.2

(Scaling Property of Relative Tube Functions)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. For any
t>0
and
\lambda>0
, we have that

V_{\lambda X,\lambda\Omega}(t)=\lambda^{N}V_{X,\Omega}(t/\lambda).

More generally, if
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a similitude with scaling ratio
\lambda
, then we have the analogous identity:

V_{\varphi(X),\varphi(\Omega)}(t)=\lambda^{N}V_{X,\Omega}(t/\lambda).

(3.1.2)

Note that this scaling property is Lemma 3.3 of
[
Hof25
]
. We reproduce the proof with slight tweaks to the notation for consistency with our present conventions.

Proof 3.1.3
.

Let
\varphi=\psi_{1}\circ\varphi_{\lambda}\circ\psi_{2}
, where
\psi_{2},\psi_{1}
are isometries (equivalently, compositions of rotations, reflections, and translations) and where
\varphi_{\lambda}
is a uniform scaling transformation, viz.
\varphi_{\lambda}(x)=\lambda x
. Any similitude of scaling ratio
\lambda
, by definition, may be written this way. Note that the first identity is a special case of the second property; merely set
\psi_{1}=\psi_{2}=\text{Id}
. For convenience, let us denote
U^{\prime}=\psi_{2}(U)
in what follows, and note that
\lambda U=\varphi_{\lambda}(U)
.

The maps in question have lots of nice properties. First, we note that
\psi_{2}
and
\psi_{1}
are isometries, which is to say that
\left|U^{\prime}\right|_{N}=\left|U\right|_{N}
and
\left|\psi_{1}(U)\right|_{N}=\left|U\right|_{N}
for any
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Since
\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\varphi_{\lambda},
and their composition
\varphi
are injective, they each have the property that the intersection of the images of two sets is the image of the intersection of those sets.
The same property is true (unconditionally) for the union of images being the image of the union.

This lattermost fact is one way to see that
\psi_{1}(X_{t})=(\psi_{1}(X))_{t}
. One may write the neighborhood
X_{t}
as a union of
t
-balls, and then apply the preservation of unions under images to see that this set is exactly the neighborhood of the transformed set
\psi_{1}(X)
.

Using this fact and the above properties, we find that the tube functions are unaffected by an injective isometry such as
\psi_{1}
:

\displaystyle V_{\psi_{1}(\lambda X^{\prime}),\psi_{1}(\lambda\Omega^{\prime})}(t)

\displaystyle=\left|\psi_{1}((\lambda X^{\prime})_{t})\cap\psi_{1}(\lambda\Omega^{\prime})\right|_{N}

\displaystyle=\left|\psi_{1}[(\lambda X^{\prime})_{t}\cap\lambda\Omega^{\prime}]\right|_{N}=V_{\lambda X^{\prime},\lambda\Omega^{\prime}}(t).

As mentioned before, we have that
\varphi_{\lambda}(X_{t})=(\lambda X)_{\lambda t}
. Writing
t=\lambda t
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle V_{\lambda X^{\prime},\lambda\Omega^{\prime}}(\lambda t)=\left|\varphi_{\lambda}(X^{\prime}_{t}\cap\Omega^{\prime})\right|_{N}=\lambda^{N}V_{X^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}}(t),

(3.1.3)

where the last step is the scaling property of the Lebesgue measure. We have already shown that for an injective isometry like
\psi_{2}
,
V_{\psi_{2}(X),\psi_{2}(\Omega)}(t)=V_{X,\Omega}(t)
. Thus, the result follows by replacing
t
in Equation
3.1.3
by
t/\lambda
and using this isometry invariance.
