This scaling property leads to a choice of normalizing factor
t^{-N}
. Since
t^{-N}\lambda^{N}=(t/\lambda)^{-N}
, if we multiply both sides of Equation
3.1.2
by this factor, then the normalized function
t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)
satisfies a homogenized scaling law.

Corollary 3.1.4

(Volume Scaling Law)
.

Let
V_{X,\Omega}
be the (relative) tube function of an RFD
(X,\Omega)
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Then the normalized (relative) tube function,
f(t;(X,\Omega)):=t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)
, satisfies the
1
-scaling law

f(t;(\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega]))=f(t/\lambda;(X,\Omega))

in the sense of Definition
2.4.2
.

3.2
Tube Zeta Functions and Complex Dimensions

Tube zeta functions were introduced in
[
LRŽ17
]
to study fractals in higher dimensions and to extend the theory of
complex dimensions
, introduced in
[
LvF13
]
for the theory of one dimensional fractal harps, to these fractals. A (relative) tube zeta function is constructed from a (relative) tube function, which is the volume of tubular neighborhoods of the given set (relative to another) as a function of the parameter. To be precise, a (relative) tube zeta function is a truncated Mellin transforms of the corresponding tube function. Consequently, these zeta functions possess important scaling properties (c.f. Lemma
3.2.4
) which will be important for studying self-similar fractals which are related to scaled copies of itself.

The complex dimensions of a set correspond to the singularities of its associated fractal zeta function. We note that there are other types of fractal zeta functions, such as distance zeta functions also introduced in
[
LRŽ17
]
. However, the key feature of a fractal zeta function is the collection of its singularities (and the residues of the function at these values), and it turns out that these two types of zeta functions are equivalent in the sense of having identical poles (with corresponding multiplicities); see Corollary 2.2.10 of
[
LRŽ17
]
. The singularities of a fractal zeta function of a set
X
are called the complex dimensions of
X
. In the case of relative tube functions, and their corresponding relative tube zeta functions, we say that these are the complex dimensions of
X
relative to the given set.

3.2.1
Relative Tube Zeta Functions

Tube zeta functions come from normalized tube functions. Note that here, we will strictly consider relative tube zeta functions, which come from relative tube functions. One may define a standard tube zeta function simply by replacement of relative tube functions with standard tube functions, or equivalently by replacement of the set
\Omega
with the ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Thus, for convenience we will sometimes simply call them tube zeta functions.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
V_{X,\Omega}
its relative tube function. We recall from Corollary
3.1.4
that the function
t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)
satisfies a homogenized scaling law. Fixing some
\delta>0
, we will take the truncated Mellin transform (see Definition
2.1.1
) of this normalized quantity to obtain the zeta function. We note that this construction suggests how to define zeta functions for quantities unrelated to volume as we shall see in Chapter
4
in the context of heat content.

Definition 3.2.1

(Relative Tube Zeta Function)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum
1
1

1

Alternatively, let
X,\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and suppose that
\Omega
has finite Lebesgue measure.
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, let
V_{X,\Omega}
its relative tube function, and let
\delta>0
. The
relative tube zeta function

\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
of
X
relative to
\Omega
is given by

\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta):=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)](s)=\int_{0}^{\delta}t^{s-N-1}V_{X,\Omega}(t)\,dt

for
s\in\mathbb{C}
with sufficiently large real part and analytically continued elsewhere in the complex plane.

Generally speaking, the analytic continuation of a (zeta) function may encounter poles, essentially singularities, or even worse (logarithmic singularities, natural boundaries, and so forth). In this work, however, we shall only have need of meromorphic functions and thus will specialize to domains in which the zeta functions only have poles for singularities. We note that in fact
\text{Re}(s)>N
is a sufficiently large real part, as the following shows.

Lemma 3.2.2

(Tube Function Asymptotics)
.

Let
X,\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Suppose that
\Omega
is measurable and that either
\Omega
has finite measure or
X
is bounded. Then the relative tube function
V_{X,\Omega}
is continuous and decreasing on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Consequently,
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta)=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)](s)
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{N}
.

Proof 3.2.3
.

The continuity and monotonicity of
V_{X,\Omega}
follow from properties of the Lebesgue measure and the containment
X_{t}\subset X_{s}
whenever
t<s
. The key element here is that
V_{X,\Omega}
is bounded as
t\to 0^{+}
, which implies that
t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)=O(t^{-N})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. This estimate not generally optimal, but is enough to ensure that tube zeta functions are well-defined. By Lemma
2.1.2
, we have that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{N}
.

Note that the choice of
\delta>0
is essentially irrelevant. For any two
\delta_{2}>\delta_{1}>0
, we have that

\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta_{2})-\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta_{1})=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}t^{s-N-1}V_{X,\Omega}(t)\,dt=\mathcal{M}_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}[t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)](s).

(3.2.1)

By Lemma
2.1.2
, this is an entire function. Thus, these two functions must have the same set of singularities and respective residues because their difference is entire. In other words, the poles of a relative tube zeta function are not dependent on the choice of
\delta
.

Tube zeta functions are well-suited to studying self-similar or nearly self-similar objects due to their scaling properties. The scaling property may be deduced directly from Lemma
2.1.6
and Corollary
3.1.4
.

Lemma 3.2.4

(Scaling Property of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an RFD in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
be its relative tube zeta function, and fix
\delta>0
. Then for any similitude
\varphi
with scaling ratio
\lambda>0
and for all
s
in the domain of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
,

\widetilde{\zeta}_{\phi(X),\phi(\Omega)}(s;\delta)=\lambda^{s}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta/\lambda)=\lambda^{s}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta)+\lambda^{s}\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda}[t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)](s).

We will see these doubly truncated transforms often enough that we given them a name,
partial tube zeta functions
, and some notation. The term partial is owing to the fact that their domain of integration does not reach zero. It is precisely the behavior near zero that matters for the development of singularities, so these functions do not detect scaling behavior in the same way that tube zeta functions do. Strictly speaking, one might call them partial
relative
tube zeta functions but we will not need this distinction.

Definition 3.2.5

(Partial (Relative) Tube Zeta Function)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
V_{X,\Omega}
its relative tube function, and let
0<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}
. A
partial (relative) tube zeta function
is the function

\widetilde{\xi}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta_{1},\delta_{2}):=\int_{\delta_{1}}^{\delta_{2}}t^{s-N-1}V_{X,\Omega}(t)\,dt.

By Lemma
2.1.2
, it is an entire function with respect to
s
.

3.2.2
Complex Dimensions

At last we may formally define complex dimensions, which lie at the heart of our analysis of self-similar fractals. First, we specify a set
W\subseteq\mathbb{C}
called a window. What we call complex dimensions are dubbed
visible
, or accessible, complex dimension in the window
W
. Secondly, we note that the definition we provide is, in a sense, only a special case. Namely, we have restricted our attention to the study of meromorphic zeta functions, having only poles for singularities. Essential singularities, or even other types of singular behavior, may yet deserve to be incorporated into the general theory. However, that is beyond the scope of the present work.

Lastly, we note that our definition of complex dimensions is for the complex dimensions of a set
X
relative to another,
\Omega
. So, these may be called the relative complex dimensions of a given set
X
(relative to
\Omega
) or more simply as the complex dimensions of the relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
itself. We will use the terminology interchangeably.

Definition 3.2.6

(Complex Dimensions of a Relative Fractal Drum)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be a relative fractal drum and let
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
be its relative tube zeta function.

Let
W\subset\mathbb{C}
such that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
is meromorphic within
W
. Then the
complex dimensions
of
X
relative to
\Omega
contained in the window
W
are the poles of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
contained within
W
. We denote them by
\mathcal{D}_{X,\Omega}(W)
, or
\mathcal{D}_{X}(W)
when the set
\Omega
is clear from context.

As alluded to earlier, the complex dimensions of a set control the exponents which appear in explicit formulae for tube functions. Namely, the exponents are codimensions of the form
N-\omega
, where
\omega
is in the set
\mathcal{D}_{X}
, and the sums are indexed by these complex dimensions. See for instance the general explicit formulae for tube functions of (generalized) fractal harps (in Chapter 5 of
[
LvF13
]
) or for relative tube functions (in Chapter 5 of
[
LRŽ17
]
).

In the present context, we will explicitly compute the possible complex dimensions of self-similar fractals using our framework of scaling functional equations, with the results having appeared in
[
Hof25
]
. In particular, Corollary
2.4.13
will produce a formula for the relative tube zeta function and a description of its possible poles, namely being governed by the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
for the self-similar system
\Phi
having
X
as its invariant set.

3.3
General Results on Self-Similar Sets

In this section, we apply the results of Chapter
2
to study self-similar fractals. The method of establishing explicit formulae for relative tube functions was established in
[
LRŽ17
]
, and the approach of explicitly computing the tube zeta functions via scaling functional equations was established in
[
Hof25
]
. Here, the main results lie in establishing formulae for the relevant fractal zeta functions and the possible complex dimensions of self-similar fractals. In particular, we will see that the self-similar system
\Phi
, whose invariant set is the given self-similar fractal
X
, controls the possible complex dimensions. Essentially, the possible complex dimensions may be deduced from only knowledge of the scaling ratios (and respective multiplicities) that define the self-similarity of the given fractal.

We note that this framework requires certain separation conditions to be well-defined, namely the introduction of an
osculant fractal drum
. Suppose that
(X,\Omega)
is a fractal drum where
X
is a self-similar set. Since an IFS with a given invariant set is not unique, the first condition (the open set condition, Definition
1.3.3
) ensures that the particular self-similar system
\Phi
carries authentic geometric information about its invariant set
X
. The second condition is that
\Omega
osculates
X
in the sense that its iterates
\varphi[\Omega]
stay closest to the corresponding iterate
\varphi[X]
of
X
itself.

This constraint is vital for establishing an induced decomposition of the relative tube functions for the fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
in terms of the tube functions for its corresponding rescaled drums,
\varphi[(X,\Omega)]:=(\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega])
. Together with the scaling properties of these volume functions, we obtain an induced scaling functional equation which may be solved to obtain explicit formulae.

3.3.1
Osculant Fractal Drums

When
X
is a self-similar set, its tubular neighborhoods
X_{t}
need not themselves be self-similar in the same sense. However, with some appropriate separation conditions imposed on a fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
, we can partition the relative tubular neighborhood for
(X,\Omega)
in terms of relative tubular neighborhoods of the similar RFDs
(\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega])
,
\varphi\in\Phi
, and a residual RFD
(X,R)
, where
R=\Omega\setminus\cup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]
. Ultimately, such a decomposition will lead to a scaling function equation for the relative tube function induced by
\Phi
, in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
. Ideally, only a small portion of this neighborhood should lie in the set
R
as this is precisely what yields the remainder term.

In order for this construction to make sense, we need the images
\varphi[\Omega]
and
\psi[\Omega]
to be disjoint whenever
\varphi\neq\psi
, where
\varphi,\psi\in\Phi
, as well as the containment
\varphi[\Omega]\subseteq\Omega
for each
\varphi\in\Phi
. Note that these are exactly the conditions so that
\Omega
is a feasible set for the open set condition for
\Phi
(see Definition
1.3.3
). This is our first condition.

Next we note a small technical point:
R
need not be open, so strictly speaking
(X,R)
is not an RFD. If
\partial R
has measure zero, we may safely replace
R
by its interior and
V_{X,R}
shall be unaffected. Even if not, however, the definition of
R
ensures that it is measurable with
\left|R\right|_{N}\leq\left|\Omega\right|_{N}<\infty
, and thus
V_{X,R}
(and consequently
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,R}
) will still be well-defined as in Definition
3.1.1
(resp. Definition
3.2.1
).

Next, we will need that
X_{t}\cap\varphi(\Omega)
is in fact
(\varphi(X))_{t}\cap\varphi(\Omega)
for each
\varphi\in\Phi
. Equivalently, for each map
\varphi\in\Phi
and every point
y\in\varphi(\Omega)
,
d(y,X)=d(y,\varphi(X))
. This condition will be necessary to relate
V_{X,\varphi(\Omega)}
to
V_{\varphi(X),\varphi(\Omega)}
. This we call the osculating condition, introduced in
[
Hof25
]
: the points in iterates
\varphi(\Omega)
of
\Omega
(
\varphi\in\Phi
) stay closest to the corresponding iterate
\varphi(X)
of
X
, rather than to a different iterate
\psi(X)
, where
\psi\in\Phi
and
\psi\neq\varphi
.

Definition 3.3.1

(Osculating Sets and Osculant Fractal Drums)
.

Let
\Phi
be an iterated function system on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
and let
X
be its attractor. A nonempty, open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is said to be an
osculating set
for
\Phi
if the following hold:

1.

\Phi
satisfies the open set condition with respect to
\Omega
;

2.

For each
\varphi\in\Phi
, if
y\in\varphi[\Omega]
, then
d(y,X)=d(y,\varphi[X])
.

A relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
is called an
osculant fractal drum
if there exists an IFS
\Phi
for which
X
is its attractor and for which
\Omega
is an osculating set thereof.

In practice, it is generally easiest to ensure that a relative fractal drum is osculant in the construction of the set
\Omega
. In Section
3.4
, we will see that the interior of an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake
(contained within a sector of angle
2\pi/n
) serves as an osculating set when
r
is chosen to be sufficiently small.

In the general setting, the following algorithm may be attempted to construct an osculating set. Note, however, that this may produce an empty set. Suppose that
X
is the attractor of an iterated function system
\Phi
. For each map
\varphi\in\Phi
, define the set

U_{\varphi}:=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\,\middle|\,d(x,\varphi[X])<\min_{\psi\in\Phi\setminus\left\{\varphi\right\}}d(x,\psi[X])\right\}.

(3.3.1)

By construction,
U_{\varphi}
consists of all the points in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
which are closest to some point of
\varphi[X]
and strictly further from any other respective image. By construction,
U_{\varphi}
is open (it may be written as the preimage of a continuous function, the difference of the distance functions, of an open interval). Note that if
\varphi[X]\subset\psi[X]
for some other map
\psi
—which means that
\Phi
will fail to satisfy the open set condition—this set is empty.

Now consider the new open set
U^{\prime}
defined by

U^{\prime}=\bigcap_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi^{-1}[U_{\varphi}].

By construction, if
x\in U^{\prime}
then
\varphi(x)\in U_{\varphi}
. Note, however, that
U^{\prime}
may or may not be nonempty in general (even if each individual
U_{\varphi}
is nonempty). The osculating condition is satisfied by
U^{\prime}
: for any
x\in U^{\prime}
and
\varphi\in\Phi
, we have that
\varphi(x)\in U_{\varphi}
, whence
d(x,\varphi[X])=d(x,X)
by definition. The set
U^{\prime}
, or some nonempty subset of it, is a candidate for an osculating set
\Omega
if it is also a feasible set for which
\Phi
satisfies the open set condition. In fact, if
\Phi
consists only of injective maps (such as is the case for self-similar systems), then it follows by construction that the pairwise images of
U^{\prime}
are disjoint. In this case, one must then check that
U^{\prime}
is nonempty and that it contains its images, i.e. for each
\varphi\in\Phi
,
\varphi[U^{\prime}]\subseteq U^{\prime}
.

3.3.2
Induced Decompositions of Osculant Fractal Drums

The conditions of an osculant fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
ensure that its relative tube function
V_{X,\Omega}
has an induced decomposition in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
. This, together with the volume scaling law (Corollary
3.1.4
), will allow us to apply the results of Chapter
2
to set up and solve scaling functional equations for the tube function.

Theorem 3.3.2

(Induced Decomposition of Relative Tube Functions)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum with respect to
\Phi
, and let
V_{X,\Omega}
be the relative tube function of
(X,\Omega)
. Let
R=\Omega\setminus\cup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]
, which we call the
residual set
.

Then
\Phi
induces a decomposition of
V_{X,\Omega}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with remainder term
V_{X,R}(t)=\left|X_{t}\cap R\right|_{N}
in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
.

We note that this result is essentially the proof of Theorem 5.3 in
[
Hof25
]
. The final step to obtain the statement of this theorem is the application of Lemma
3.1.2
, which we state here as a separate corollary.

Proof 3.3.3
.

We begin by partitioning
\Omega
according to the iterates and the residual set:

\Omega=\left(\biguplus_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]\right)\uplus R.

Observe that this is a partition since
\Omega
is an osculating set for
\Phi
(and by the definition of
R
). Next, we intersect each of the sets of the partition with
X_{t}
and take the Lebesgue measure. By disjoint additivity, we obtain that

V_{X,\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}V_{X,\varphi[\Omega]}(t)+V_{X,R}(t),

where
V_{X,R}(t)=\left|X_{t}\cap R\right|_{N}
. It is not strictly speaking a relative tube function; however,
R
is measurable, so it is well-defined. To see this, note that
\Omega
is measurable and so too is each set
\varphi[\Omega]
. The easiest way to see that
\varphi[\Omega]
is measurable is to note that a similitude is injective, whence its inverse is well-defined and continuous, in which case
\varphi[\Omega]
is the preimage of
\Omega
under a continuous (hence measurable) function.

By the osculant condition, we have that for each
\varphi\in\Phi
,
d(y,X)=d(y,\varphi[X])
for all
y\in\varphi[\Omega]
. Consequently,
X_{t}\cap\varphi[\Omega]=(\varphi[X])_{t}\cap\varphi[\Omega]
, as each of these neighborhoods
X_{t}
is the preimage of the interval
[0,t)
under the respective distance functions,
d(\cdot,X)\equiv d(\cdot,\varphi[X])
, equivalent in
\varphi[\Omega]
by assumption. Thus,
V_{X,\varphi[\Omega]}=V_{\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega]}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Putting everything together, we obtain the identity that

V_{X,\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}V_{\varphi[X],\varphi[\Omega]}(t)+V_{X,R}(t),

which is exactly an induced decomposition.

Given an induced decomposition and the scaling law from Lemma
3.1.2
, we obtain an induced scaling functional equation for
V_{X,\Omega}
in the sense of Proposition
2.4.3
.

Corollary 3.3.4

(Induced Scaling Functional Equation)
.

Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system, let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum with respect to
\Phi
, and let
V_{X,\Omega}
be the relative tube function of
(X,\Omega)
. Let
R=\Omega\setminus\cup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]
be the residual set and let
V_{X,R}(t)=\left|X_{t}\cap R\right|_{N}
.

Then
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
V_{X,\Omega}=L_{\Phi}[V_{X,\Omega}]+V_{X,R}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
, where
L_{\Phi}
is the scaling operator associated to
\Phi
as in Equation
2.2.2
.

3.3.3
Complex Dimensions

In order to apply the results of Chapter
2
, we must have some control over the remainder term,
V_{X,R}
, that appears in the induced functional equation of Corollary
3.3.4
. Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum with respect to a self-similar system
\Phi
, and let
R=\Omega\setminus\cup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]
and
V_{X,R}
be defined accordingly.

Our main type of estimate is when there exists some
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}
such that at
t\to 0^{+}
, we have that
R(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}})
. Then
t^{-N}V_{x,R}(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
and thus by Theorem
2.4.5
it is an admissible remainder for the scaling functional equation
V_{X,\Omega}=L_{\Phi}[V_{X,\Omega}]+V_{X,R}
for any screen of the form
S(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon>0
arbitrarily small.

Given such an estimate, we can deduce an explicit formula for the relative tube zeta function of
(X,\Omega)
, and thus the possible complex dimensions as well as explicit tube formulae in either a pointwise or distributional sense.

Theorem 3.3.5

(Tube Zeta Function Formula)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with corresponding self-similar system
\Phi
. Let
V_{X,\Omega}
be its relative tube function and let
V_{X,R}
be the relative tube function of the residual set (as in Theorem
3.3.2
). Suppose that as
t\to 0^{+}
,
V_{X,R}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}})
.

Then the relative tube zeta function of
(X,\Omega)
is given by

\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{\Phi}(s)(\xi_{\Phi,f}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{X,R}(s;\delta)),

(3.3.2)

where
\xi_{\Phi,f}
is defined as in Equation
2.4.3
with
f(t)=t^{-N}V_{X,\Omega}(t)
. Letting
D=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
, we have that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta)
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{D}
. Further,
\xi_{\Phi,f}
is an entire function and
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
, whereby
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
admits a meromorphic continuation to
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
, having poles contained in the set
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
. If
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
, then
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})=\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof 3.3.6
.

First, we note that by Theorem
3.3.2
,
\Phi
induces a decomposition of
V_{X,\Omega}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
with remainder term
R
. By Corollary
3.1.4
, we have that
V_{X,\Omega}
satisfies a
1
-scaling law, so by Proposition
2.4.3
,
\Phi
induces the scaling functional equation
V_{X,\Omega}=L_{\Phi}[V_{X,\Omega}]+R
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Thus, by Corollary
2.4.13
(with
\alpha=1
), we have that Equation
3.3.2
holds and that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{D}
and meromorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
, having poles contained in
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
. If we have that
\sigma_{0}<D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
, we have that
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})=\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{C})
because all the poles
\omega
of
\zeta_{\Phi}
have
D_{\ell}\leq\text{Re}(\omega)\leq D
.

From this theorem, we can describe the possible complex dimensions of the fractal drum explicitly, as they are governed by the explicit function
\zeta_{\Phi}
. Note, however, that it is possible for the residue of the tube function to vanish at a pole
\omega
of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, in which case that it is not a complex dimension of
(X,\Omega)
. This occurs if
\xi_{\Phi,f}(\omega;\delta)+\zeta_{X,R}(\omega;\delta)=0
and vanishes to a degree equal to or larger than than the multiplicity of the pole
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
.

Corollary 3.3.7

(Possible Complex Dimensions)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with corresponding self-similar system
\Phi
and suppose that the hypotheses of Theorem
3.3.5
hold.

Then the set
\mathcal{D}_{X}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
of complex dimensions of
X
relative to
\Omega
in the window
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
satisfies the containment

\mathcal{D}_{X}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})\subset\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})=\Big\{\omega\in\mathbb{C}:1=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{\omega}\Big\}.

(3.3.3)

A point
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
is a complex dimension of
X
(relative to
\Omega
) exactly when
\Res(\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega)\neq 0
. If
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
, then
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})=\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}({\mathbb{C}})
.

Of note, Theorem
3.3.7
requires a suitable estimate for the remainder to produce any results. For example, the knowledge that
V_{X,R}
is bounded yields no information, as the estimate corresponds to
\sigma_{0}=N
and in
\mathbb{H}_{N}
,
\zeta_{\Phi}
is holomorphic since
\dim_{S}(\Phi)\leq N
. The result in general requires that there exists a screen on which
\zeta_{\Phi}
and
\zeta_{R}
are both jointly languid. Imposing that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
is tantamount to imposing that the remainder is holomorphic in a half-plane which contains all of the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
, which are contained in the strip defined by
\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)\leq\text{Re}(s)\leq\dim_{S}(\Phi)
.

It is possible to extend the result even if this is not possible, but it requires knowledge of the locations of the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
in the form of dimension-free regions. This is easily done in the lattice case (see Definition
1.1.4
), but is in general a much more challenging problem in the generic nonlattice case.

The best results occur if the parameter
\sigma_{0}
is small; in Section
3.4
, our results shall apply for any order
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently close to
0
. Informally,
\sigma_{0}
corresponds to the (Minkowski) dimension of the remainder quantity: when
V_{X,R}
scales like the volume of an epsilon neighborhood of a point, the exponent is
N
and thus
\sigma_{0}=0
; when
V_{X,R}
scales like the volume of a neighborhood of a line, so the exponent is
N-1
and
\sigma_{0}=1
; and so forth. (Recall the discussion in Subsection
3.1.2
.) In other words, our work determines the complex dimensions with real parts (or amplitudes) up to the real part corresponding to the dimension of (the estimate of) the remainder term.

3.3.4
Explicit Formulae

Having established the identities for the tube zeta functions and deduced the possible complex dimensions, we may now proceed to proving explicit formulae for the relative tube functions themselves. First, though, we recall some notation from Section
2.5
adapted to the present context. Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with associated self-similar system
\Phi
.

Firstly, define
V_{X,\Omega}^{[0]}:=V_{X,\Omega}
. For any
k>0
, we define the
k^{\text{th}}
antiderivative
V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]}
recursively by integrating the previous antiderivative and imposing the convention that
f^{[k]}(0)=0
. That is, for
k>0
,

V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}V_{X,\Omega}^{[k-1]}(\tau)\,d\tau.

Next, we recall that the Pochhammer symbol is defined by
(z)_{w}:=\Gamma(z+w)/\Gamma(w)
for
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
. In the special case that
w=k
is a positive integer, this simplifies to
(z)_{k}=z(z+1)\cdots(z+k-1)
. If
w=0
, then we have that
(z)_{0}=1
.

Lastly, we recall an important caveat regarding the nature of the sums which will appear forthwith. Namely, they are symmetric limits and thus may have delicate convergence. Letting
\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{C}
denote a discrete subset with the property that for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{D}_{m}:=\left\{\omega\in\mathcal{D}\,\middle|\,|\text{Im}(\omega)|\leq m\right\}<\infty.

We define

\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}}a_{\omega}:=\lim_{m\to\infty}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{m}}a_{\omega}.

In what follows,
\mathcal{D}
will be the set of poles of the zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
contained in a vertical strip. When the imaginary parts are bounded, the region is thus also finite. It is an elementary fact (following from the identity theorem) that a meromorphic function can have only finitely many poles in a bounded, connected region, so the sums over the set of poles of
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
are well-defined.

Theorem 3.3.8

(Pointwise Explicit Tube Formula)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with corresponding self-similar system
\Phi
. Let
V_{X,\Omega}
be its relative tube function and let
V_{X,R}
be the relative tube function of the residual set (as in Theorem
3.3.2
). Suppose that as
t\to 0^{+}
,
V_{X,R}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}})
. Suppose either that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
(see Definition
2.2.9
) or that the scaling ratios of
\Phi
are arithmetically related (see Definition
1.1.4
).

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that

V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{N-s+k}}{(N-s+1)_{k}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
is given explicitly by Theorem
3.3.5
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof 3.3.9
.

This theorem is a corollary of Theorem
2.5.1
from Chapter
2
. Note that
\beta=N
,
\alpha=1
, and
N+1>\dim_{S}(\Phi)\geq\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)>\sigma_{0}
.

The induced scaling functional equation, with the scaling operator
L_{\Phi}
, is the result of Corollary
3.3.4
. The condition that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
is a sufficient condition for the admissibility of the remainder term and any screen of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon
per Theorem
2.4.5
, together with the presupposed estimate for
V_{X,R}
. In the event that the scaling ratios are arithmetically related, instead we may apply Theorem
2.4.9
to deduce admissibility of the remainder and screens
S_{\varepsilon}
. Note that
W_{S_{\varepsilon}}=\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}
is simply a half-plane.

In both cases,
\varepsilon
is chosen so that
|\text{Re}(\omega)-\sigma_{0}-\varepsilon|>0
for any
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
. (Either we choose
\varepsilon<D_{\ell}-\sigma_{0}
or
\varepsilon
to be smaller than the minimum distance to any of the finitely many exceptional real parts.) By the estimate for
V_{X,R}
, we have that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{R}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
and
\sup(S)=\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon>\sigma_{0}
, so
S_{\varepsilon}
is contained in the half plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}=\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
.

Lastly, we note that in these specific cases, we may reindex by the set
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
rather than
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon}
for uniformity. This follows because
\zeta_{\Phi}
, and thus
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
, has no poles with real part
\text{Re}(s)\in(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}+\varepsilon]
by our choice of
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.

Next, we recall the theory of Schwartz distributions. For our test functions, let
\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
be the space of
functions of rapid decrease, given by

\mathcal{S}(0,\delta):=\left\{\psi\in C^{\infty}(0,\delta)\,\middle|\,\begin{aligned} &\forall m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\forall q\in\mathbb{N},\text{ as }t\to 0^{+},\\
&t^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\text{ and }(t-\delta)^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\,\end{aligned}\right\}.

(3.3.4)

The space of Schwartz distributions is the dual space,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
. For a distribution
F\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
and a test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
, we write
F(\psi)=\langle F,\psi\rangle
. Two distributions
F,G
are said to be equal if for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,
\langle F,\psi\rangle=\langle G,\psi\rangle
.

Given a test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
and
a>0
, define the new test function
\psi_{a}(t):=a^{-1}\psi(t/a)
. A distribution
\mathcal{R}
is said to be
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{\alpha})
as
t\to 0^{+}
if for all
a>0
and for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\langle\mathcal{R},\psi_{a}(t)\rangle=O(a^{\alpha}),

(3.3.5)

as
t\to 0^{+}
in the usual sense.

Theorem 3.3.10

(Distributional Explicit Tube Formula)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with corresponding self-similar system
\Phi
. Let
V_{X,\Omega}
be its relative tube function and let
V_{X,R}
be the relative tube function of the residual set (as in Theorem
3.3.2
). Suppose that as
t\to 0^{+}
,
V_{X,R}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}})
. Suppose either that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
(see Definition
2.2.9
) or that the scaling ratios of
\Phi
are arithmetically related (see Definition
1.1.4
).

Then for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that for
t\in(0,\delta)
,
V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]}
satisfies

\begin{split}V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]}(t)&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{N-s+k}}{(N-s+1)_{k}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{[k]}(t),\end{split}

(3.3.6)

in the sense of distributions. See Equation
3.3.7
for the explicit identity of action on test functions. Here,
\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}
is given explicitly by Theorem
3.3.5
and the distributional remainder term satisfies the estimate
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in the sense of Equation
3.3.5
, for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Most precisely, Equation
3.3.6
means that for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\begin{split}\langle V_{X,\Omega}^{[k]},\psi\rangle&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{\mathcal{M}[\psi]({N-s+k+1})}{(N-s+1)_{k}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s);\omega\Bigg)+\langle\mathcal{R}^{[k]},\psi\rangle.\end{split}

(3.3.7)

Proof 3.3.11
.

This theorem is a corollary of Theorem
2.5.2
from Chapter
2
. The application of the theorem follows the same comments and justifications as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.8
in regards to the induced scaling functional equation, values of the parameters, admissibility of the remainder, and so forth.

Corollary 3.3.12

(Distributional Explicit Tube Formula,
k=0
)
.

Let
(X,\Omega)
be an osculant fractal drum in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with corresponding self-similar system
\Phi
be such that the hypotheses of Theorem
3.3.10
holds.

Then we have that for
t\in(0,\delta)
,
V_{X,\Omega}
satisfies

\begin{split}V_{X,\Omega}(t)&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg({t^{N-s}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{[k]}(t),\end{split}

(3.3.8)

in the sense of distributions, and the remainder satisfies the estimate
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in the sense of Equation
3.3.5
with
k=0
, for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

If we further assume that the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
are simple, then Equation
3.3.8
simplifies to

V_{X,\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res(\widetilde{\zeta}_{X,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega)\,t^{N-\omega}+\mathcal{R}(t).

3.4
Examples: Generalized von Koch Fractals

In this section, we shall apply the results of Section
3.3
to generalized von Koch fractals (or GKFs.) See Section
1.4
for the definitions and properties concerning these fractals. In particular, we provide a scaling functional equation for the tubular neighborhood volume and estimates of the error term that allow us to describe the possible complex dimensions of these GKFs.

A tube formula (see Definition
3.1.1
) for the standard von Koch snowflake was established in the work of Lapidus and Pearse
[
LP06
]
. They computed that the volume of an inner epsilon neighborhood of the von Koch snowflake takes the form

V(\varepsilon)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}\varepsilon^{2-D-inP}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi_{n}\varepsilon^{2-inP},

where
D=\log_{3}4
is the Minkowski dimension of the snowflake,
P=2\pi/\log 3
is the multiplicative period of the oscillations, and with
\psi_{n},\phi_{n}
as constants depending only on
n

[
LP06
]
. As a consequence, they deduced the possible complex (fractal) dimensions of the von Koch snowflake. These complex dimensions encode both the amplitude and period of geometric oscillations in a fractal, and are pivotal to establishing such tube formulae in the work of Lapidus and collaborators; see
[
LvF13
,
LRŽ17
]
and the references therein.

In
[
Hof25
]
, we extended this analysis to general
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflakes, where
r
is chosen to be sufficiently small, using the methods which have been generalized here in Chapter
2
. In particular, we deduced the possible complex dimensions of these GKFs with positive real part. In this setting, the lattice/nonlattice dichotomy arises depending on whether the scaling ratio
r
and its conjugate factor
\ell=\frac{1}{2}(1-r)
are arithmetically related or not (see Definition
1.1.4
). For the standard
(3,\frac{1}{3})
-von Koch snowflake, the ratios are the same:
r=\ell
, and thus the fractal falls under the lattice case. The emergence of the nonlattice case is unique to the generalizations of the von Koch snowflake.

3.4.1
Geometric Preliminaries

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be a
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake which is a simple, closed curve.
2
2

2

See Proposition
1.4.3
for a sufficient condition based on
r
and
n
.
By the Jordan curve theorem, a simple closed curve bisects the plane into two connected components, one of which is bounded and the other unbounded. Let us denote by
\Omega
the bounded component; then
\partial\Omega={K_{n,r}}
. By Proposition
1.4.3
, we have an explicit upper bound on
r
which is a sufficient condition for this to hold.

(a)

(b)

Figure 3.2:
Inner tubular neighborhoods of the fractals
K_{3,\frac{1}{5}}
(left) and
K_{4,\frac{1}{5}}
(right).

Note that
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
is readily seen to be a relative fractal drum since the sets are both bounded. We shall be interested in describing the inner tubular neighborhood of
{K_{n,r}}
, which is the tube function of
{K_{n,r}}
relative to the set
\Omega
. See Figure
3.2
for two such examples.

We note that by the
n
-fold symmetry of these regions, it is enough to compute a formula for the portion of the neighborhood contained in a sector of angle
2\pi/n
, chosen so that the rays defining the section pass through the edges of the regular
n
-gon used to define the generalized von Koch fractal. So, let
S
be any one of the
n
congruent (open) sectors defined by the center of
{K_{n,r}}
and two adjacent vertices of this underlying regular
n
-gon. For simplicity of notation, we define the new relative tube function by

V_{K,U}(t):=V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega\cap S}(t)=\frac{1}{n}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t),

defined for the RFD
(K,U)=({K_{n,r}},\Omega\cap S)
. Note that
K
is precisely an
(n,r)
-von Koch curve. Let
{\Phi_{n,r}}
be the explicit self-similar system defined in Equation
1.4.2
and, up to application of an isometry, we may without loss of generality assume that
K
is the attractor of
{\Phi_{n,r}}
.

Figure 3.3:
An inner tubular neighborhood of a
(3,\frac{1}{5})
-von Koch snowflake. A relative neighborhood in one sector of angle
2\pi/3
is shaded in the bottom left and images of the relative neighborhood in the top third, under mappings of the self-similar system, are shaded in the top. Note the leftover portion of the inner tubular neighborhood contained in the residual set.

If we can show that this is an osculant fractal drum and appropriately estimate the remainder, then we will be able to apply the results of Section
3.3
. We may partition
K_{t}\cap U
into
2+(n-1)
self-similar copies of itself as well as
4
pieces contained within congruent triangles and
2
pieces which are exactly circular sectors. See Figure
3.3
for a depiction of such a decomposition. This partitioning leads to the following theorem.

Theorem 3.4.1

(SFE for an
(n,r)
-von Koch Curve)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Let
S
be a sector of angle
2\pi/n
centered in the regular
n
-gon used to define
{K_{n,r}}
and with edges passing through two adjacent vertices of this polygon.

Then
(K,U)=({K_{n,r}},\Omega\cap S)
is an osculant fractal drum with respect to the self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}
. For any
t\geq 0
, we that
V_{K,U}
satisfies the scaling functional equation

t^{-2}V_{K,U}(t)=2(t/\ell)^{-2}V_{K,U}(t/\ell)+(n-1)(t/r)^{-2}V_{K,U}(t/r)+t^{-2}V_{K,R}(t),

(3.4.1)

where
\ell=\frac{1}{2}(1-r)
is the conjugate scaling factor to
r
. We have that
V_{K,R}(t)=O(t^{2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
with the explicit estimate that
V_{K,R}(t)(t)\leq(2\cot(\theta_{n}/2)+\theta_{n})t^{2}
for any
t\geq 0
. Here,
\theta_{n}=\frac{2\pi}{n}
is the central angle of a regular
n
-gon.

Proof 3.4.2
.

Without loss of generality, we may suppose that
K:={K_{n,r}}\cap S
is exactly the
(n,r)
-von Koch curve
{C_{n,r}}
; in general,
K
is isometric to this set. In this setting, we have explicitly the similitudes for the self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}=\left\{\phi_{L},\phi_{R},\psi_{k},k=1,...,n-1\right\}
which defines
{C_{n,r}}
as in Definition
1.4.1
. (In the general setting, simply compose these maps
\varphi\in{\Phi_{n,r}}
with the isometry between the sets.)

By definition,
K
is the attractor of the self-similar system
\Phi
in Definition
1.4.1
. There are two similitudes
\phi_{L}
and
\phi_{R}
with scaling ratios
\ell:=\frac{1}{2}(1-r)\in(0,1)
and
n-1
similitudes
\psi_{k}
with scaling ratios
r\in(0,1)
. So, we define the scaling operator
L=L_{n,r}
by
L:=2M_{\ell}+(n-1)M_{r}.

Let
U=\Omega\cap S
. Then we have that
U
is an osculating set for
\Phi
. Indeed, the collection of sets
\left\{\phi_{L}[U],\phi_{R}[U],\psi_{k}[U],k=1,...,n-1\right\}
is readily seen to be pairwise disjoint and each set is contained within
U
itself.
(K,U)
is a readily seen to be a relative fractal drum since
K
is contained in the bound of
U
and
U
is a bounded, measurable set.

The osculating condition may be seen from the symmetry involved in the construction of
K
. In particular, we shall use the property that if
X
and
X^{\prime}
are two sets in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
related by reflection about the line
L
, then the set of points which are equidistant to
X
and
X^{\prime}
must lie on
L
. We note that by choice of
U
, its images
\psi_{k}[U]
partition a regular
n
-gon scaled by a factor of
r
about the lines of symmetry passing from the center of the shape to its vertices. Because the adjacent images
\psi_{j}[K]
and
\psi_{j+1}[K]
are reflections of each other about these lines, it follows that
d(\cdot,K)=\min_{\phi\in\Phi}d(\cdot,\phi[K])
can only change form across these lines by continuity of the distance functions. In other words, it follows that
d(\cdot,K)\equiv d(\cdot,\psi_{j}[K])
in each
\psi_{j}[U]
,
j=1,...,n-1
.

Now, the other two iterates on the edges are simpler. Indeed, we have that
d(\cdot,K)\equiv d(\cdot,\phi_{L}[K])
in
\phi_{L}[U]
(resp.
d(\cdot,K)\equiv d(\cdot,\phi_{R}[K])
in
\phi_{R}[U]
) is clear from the geometry of
K
since a ball
B_{\varepsilon}(y)
for
y\in\phi_{L}[U]
(resp.
y\in\phi_{R}[U]
) will intersect with
\phi_{L}[K]
(resp.
\phi_{R}[K])
before ever reaching
\psi_{1}[K]
(resp.
\psi_{n-1}[K]
).

It follows by Corollary
3.3.4
that
t^{-2}V_{K,U}(t)
satisfies the scaling functional equation

t^{-2}V_{K,U}(t)=2(t/\ell)^{-2}V_{K,U}(t/\ell)+(n-1)(t/r)^{-2}V_{K,U}(t/r)+t^{-2}V_{K,R}(t),

where
R=U\setminus(\cup_{\phi\in\Phi}\phi[U])
and
V_{K,R}(t)=\left|K_{t}\cap R\right|_{N}
. We note that
K_{t}\cap R
may be partitioned into two sectors of angle
\theta_{n}
and length
\varepsilon
and four congruent pieces contained in triangles of height
t
and width
t\cot(\theta_{n}/2)
. See Figure
3.4
for a depiction of two such triangles. Therefore, we may write that

R(\varepsilon):=V_{K,R}(t)\leq\theta_{n}t^{2}+2t^{2}\cot(\theta_{n}/2).

Figure 3.4:
A portion of the tubular neighborhood of the von Koch curve
C_{3,1/3}
is depicted. In particular, note the regions contained in the triangles of height
t
and width
t\cot(\theta_{n}/2)
, where
\theta_{n}=2\pi/n
, on the left and the right of the figure. These are used as part of an estimate the volume of the relative tubular neighborhood of a von Koch snowflake contained in the residual set.

Because we have that
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=nV_{K,U}(t)
, we obtain the following scaling functional equation for the relative fractal drum of the boundary of the fractal snowflake relative to its interior as an immediate corollary. Note that the remainder term is also scaled by a factor of
n
but otherwise satisfies the same estimate.

Corollary 3.4.3

(SFE for an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
).

Then the tube function
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
of the relative fractal drum
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
satisfies the scaling functional equation

t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=L_{\Phi_{n,r}}[t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)](t)+t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)

where
{\Phi_{n,r}}
is as in Equation
1.4.2
. The remainder term is continuous and satisfies the estimate
|V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)|\leq(2n\cot(\pi/n)+2\pi)t^{2}
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Next, we consider the lower similarity dimension of generalized von Koch snowflakes. These depend only on
n
and
r
, since
\ell=(1-r)/2
. Since our remainder estimate is of the form
t^{-2}V_{K,R}(t)=O(t^{0})
(as
t\to 0^{+}
), in order to apply the results of Section
3.3
without further assumption, we need information about the lower similarity dimension,
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
. Because
\sigma_{0}=0
and
r<\ell
(or in the case when
r=\ell=\frac{1}{3}
), it turns out that whether or not
D_{\ell}>0
is independent of the value of
r
, depending only one the multiplicities, and thus only on
n
. We have that when
n\geq 5
,
D\ell>0
, when
n=4
,
D_{\ell}=0
, and when
n=3
,
D_{\ell}<0
.

In the latter two cases, we will need to assume that the real parts of the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
are not dense in a right hand neighborhood of the origin so that screens of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\varepsilon
exist on which
\zeta_{\Phi}
is jointly languid with the remainder zeta function. Assuming that the ratios are arithmetically related, viz. assuming the lattice case, is sufficient. Notably, in the case of GKFs, it is actually possible to show that the remainder admits a meromorphic extension to the entire complex plane with a (jointly) strongly languid remainder zeta function, in which case this assumption may be dispensed with. This will be the subject of a future work.

Lemma 3.4.4

(Sufficient Lower Dimension Bounds)
.

Let
n\geq 3
,
r\in(0,\frac{1}{3}]
, and define
\ell=(1-r)/2
. Let
\Phi
a self similar system having scaling ratios
r
with multiplicity
n-1
and
\ell
with multiplicity
2
, such as
{\Phi_{n,r}}
as in Equation
1.4.2
. Denote by
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
. Then we have the following.

\displaystyle\text{If }n=3,\,D_{\ell}<0.

\displaystyle\text{If }n=4,\,D_{\ell}=0.

\displaystyle\text{If }n\geq 5,\,D_{\ell}>0.

Proof 3.4.5
.

Let
\zeta_{\Phi}
the zeta function of
\Phi
and let
P(s)
denote its denominator. Because
r\leq\frac{1}{3}
, we have that
r\leq\ell
. Suppose first that
r=\ell=\frac{1}{3}
. Then we have that

P(s)=1-2\ell^{s}-(n-1)r^{s}=1-(n+1)3^{-s}.

We may explicitly solve to find that
P(\omega)=0
when
(n+1)=3^{\omega}
, or
\omega=\log_{3}(n+1)+2\pi ik/\log 3
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
n\geq 3
, the real part of these poles are always positive and exactly equal to
\log_{3}(n+1)
. Thus,
0<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)=\log_{3}(n+1)
.

Now suppose that
r<\frac{1}{3}
, in which case
r<\ell
. Consider the polynomial

p(t)=\frac{1}{n-1}\left(r^{-1}\right)^{t}+\frac{2}{n-1}\left(\frac{\ell}{r}\right)^{t};

by Definition
2.2.9
,
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
is the unique real solution to
p(D_{\ell})=1
. Note that
p
is strictly increasing, so if
p(t)<1
, then
t<D_{\ell}
. We have that
p(0)=\frac{3}{n-1}
. If
n\geq 5
, then
p(0)<1
. If
n=4
,
p(0)=1
and thus
D_{\ell}=0
. If
n=3
,
p(0)>1
whence
D_{\ell}<0
.

3.4.2
Complex Dimensions of GKFs

Now we will deduce the possible complex dimensions of generalized von Koch fractals. In this work, we will assume that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related. In the case of two scaling ratios, this condition explicitly means that the ratio of their logarithms must be rational:

\frac{\log\ell}{\log r}=\frac{\log{(\frac{1-r}{2})}}{\log{r}}=\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}.

This ratio, viewed as a function of
r
, is continuous and strictly decreasing, taking every value in
(0,2]
over the interval
(0,\frac{1}{2}]
; see Figure
3.5
for a plot of this function. Thus, by the density of the rational numbers, for any desired
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
we may find a choice of
r_{0}
arbitrarily close to
r
for which the corresponding
(n,r_{0})
-von Koch snowflake is in the lattice case.

Figure 3.5:
A plot of
\log\ell/\log r
, the ratio of logarithms of the scaling ratios, where
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
. It is strictly increasing with range
(0,2]
, taking the value
1
when
r=\ell=\frac{1}{3}
and
2
when
r=\frac{1}{2}
. As
r\to 0^{+}
, it converges to zero.

Under these assumptions, we may apply the general results for self-similar fractals to deduce the possible complex dimensions of an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake and to compute explicit formulae for their relative tube functions. First, however, we observe a property in the lattice case regarding the poles of the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
which will have consequences for the complex dimensions and the explicit formulae of the corresponding
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflakes.

Proposition 3.4.6

(Simplicity of Poles in the Lattice Case)
.

Let
r\in(0,\frac{1}{3}]
, let
\ell=(1-r)/2
, and let
n\geq 3
. If
n=4
, suppose further that
r<\frac{1}{3}
.
3
3

3

Note that this is automatically satisfied when we impose the self-avoidance condition in Proposition
1.4.3
.
If
r
and
\ell
are arithmetically related, then the poles of the function

\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}(s)=\cfrac{1}{1-(n-1)r^{s}-2\ell^{s}}

are all simple.

Proof 3.4.7
.

This follows from the proof of Proposition 1.3 (i) in
[
vdB00b
]
with the following modifications regarding the notation and the definition of the polynomial used therein.

Let
P(s)=\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}(s)^{-1}
. A pole
\omega
of
\zeta_{\Phi}
occurs with multiplicity two or greater if we have that both
P(\omega)=0
and
P^{\prime}(\omega)=0
. In the lattice case, let
p
and
q
be such that
r=r_{0}^{q}
and
\ell=r_{0}^{p}
(so that
\log\ell/\log r=p/q
) and define
z=r_{0}^{\omega}
. When
p=q
, we have that
r=\ell=\frac{1}{3}
and thus
P(s)=1-(n+1)3^{-s}
and
P^{\prime}(s)=(n+1)3^{-s}\log 3
. It follows immediately that
P^{\prime}(s)\neq 0
by properties of the complex exponential, so we may assume that
p\neq q
.

Solving now for when
P(\omega)=0
and
P^{\prime}(\omega)=0
, we obtain the system of equations:

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=(n-1)z^{q}+2z^{p},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(n-1)qz^{q}+2pz^{p}.

Solving the system yields the relations that

\displaystyle z^{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n-1)(1-q/p)},

\displaystyle z^{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(1-p/q)}.

By considering the quantity
z^{-pq}
, we obtain the equations

\displaystyle(n-1)^{p}(1-q/p)^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{q}(1-p/q)^{q},

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}q^{q}(n-1)^{p}(q-p)^{p}

\displaystyle=2^{q}p^{p}(q-p)^{q}.

Note that this last identity is equivalent to Equation 3.6 in
[
vdB00b
]
. There are no solutions when
q>p
. If
p<q
, then the only solution occurs when
n=4
,
p=3
, and
q=2
. Because we enforce that
r<\frac{1}{3}
, we have that
r<\ell
and thus that
\log\ell/\log r=p/q<1
, reaching the same contradiction as in
[
vdB00b
]
.

We now apply our results to deduce the possible complex dimensions of generalized von Koch fractals. We observe that since the relative tube function
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=nV_{K,U}(t)
, its normalization satisfies the same scaling functional equation (with remainder also scaled by
n
). Consequently, it will have the same possible complex dimensions, so we state our result for the
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake itself.

Theorem 3.4.8

(Complex Dimensions of
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflakes)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
).

Then the complex dimensions
\mathcal{D}_{K_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})
satisfy the containment

\mathcal{D}_{K_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{C}\,\middle|\,1=2((1-r)/2)^{\omega}+(n-1)r^{\omega}\right\},

where
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})
is the set of possible complex dimensions.

Proof 3.4.9
.

By Corollary
3.4.3
, we have that
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
is an osculant fractal drum and that its relative tube function
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
satisfies a scaling functional equation of the form

t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=L_{\Phi_{n,r}}[t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)](t)+t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t),

where
{\Phi_{n,r}}
is as in Equation
1.4.2
and where
t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)
is continuous with estimate
t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)=O(t^{0})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

We may follow the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.5
, namely applying Corollary
2.4.13
, to deduce that the corresponding tube function satisfies the identity

\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}(s)(\xi_{n,r}(s;\delta)+\widetilde{\zeta}_{R}(s;\delta))

(3.4.2)

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{R}=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)](s)
and where
\xi_{n,r}:=\xi_{L_{\Phi_{n,r}},t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}}
.

The result follows just as in Corollary
3.3.7
, with respect to the self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}
and
\sigma_{0}=0
.

Figure 3.6:
Two dimensional complex argument plots of associated scaling zeta functions of the fractals
K_{3,\frac{1}{3}}
(left),
K_{4,\frac{1}{4}}
(middle), and
K_{5,\frac{1}{5}}
(right). The possible complex dimensions of the fractals occur at the poles of these functions, indicated in the plots by the white circles.

Using this result, we may compute explicitly the possible complex dimensions of an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake using the corresponding (complexified) Moran equation alone. See Figure
3.6
for some plots of the scaling zeta functions
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
, whose poles determine the possible complex dimensions, as well as the corresponding
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflakes.

3.4.3
Tube Formulae for GKFs

In this subsection, we deduce explicit tube formulae for
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflakes. In what follows, we will assume that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related. We also impose the self-avoidance criterion (Proposition
1.4.3
) for
r
as well. The latter case ensures that
{K_{n,r}}
has a connected interior region
\Omega
which we use to construct its relative fractal drum
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
. The former restriction ensures that the scaling functional equations satisfied by the relative tube function
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
have admissible remainders.

For the preliminaries regarding the notation and conventions defining the antiderivatives
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}^{[k]}
, the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{w}
,
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, and the definition of the sums over complex dimensions as symmetric limits, we refer the reader to Section
3.3
.

Theorem 3.4.10

(Pointwise Tube Formulae for GKFs)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (viz. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
, any
\delta>0
, and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that

V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{2-s+k}}{(2-s+1)_{k}}\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
is given explicitly by Equation
3.4.2
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{2-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof 3.4.11
.

We have by Corollary
3.4.3
that the tube function
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
of the relative fractal drum
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
satisfies the scaling functional equation

t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=L_{\Phi_{n,r}}[t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)](t)+t^{-2}V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)

where
{\Phi_{n,r}}
is as in Equation
1.4.2
. The remainder term is continuous and satisfies the estimate
|V_{{K_{n,r}},R}(t)|\leq(2n\cot(\pi/n)+2\pi)\,t^{2}
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

We note that
({K_{n,r}},\Omega)
is strictly speaking not an osculant fractal drum with respect to the self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}
because this requires that
{K_{n,r}}
is the invariant set of
{\Phi_{n,r}}
. Instead, each of the
n
congruent components of
{K_{n,r}}
is isometric to its attractor, an
(n,r)
-von Koch curve. However, because we have a scaling functional equation (established by linearity), the proof of Theorem
3.3.8
is essentially unchanged as we apply Theorem
2.5.1
in the same manner.

As to the admissibility of the remainder with respect to the screens
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\varepsilon>0
, we apply Lemma
3.4.4
when
n\geq 5
to ensure that
\sigma_{0}=0<\underline{\dim}_{S}({\Phi_{n,r}})
which is a sufficient condition per Theorem
2.4.5
. In the lattice case, we use Theorem
2.4.9
.

Note that in the lattice case, we have by Proposition
3.4.6
that the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
are simple. Thus, we may simplify the residues to obtain an expansion in powers of
t^{2-\omega+k}
.

Corollary 3.4.12

(Pointwise Tube Formulae for GKFs, Lattice Case)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that its scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
, any
\delta>0
, and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that

V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\cfrac{r_{\omega}}{(2-\omega+1)_{k}}\,t^{2-\omega+k}+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
r_{\omega}
is a constant given by
r_{\omega}:=\Res\left(\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\right)
, with
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
given explicitly by Equation
3.4.2
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{2-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

We note that the shift by one in the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{k}
can be understood in a straightforward manner. If we use its expansion as a product when
k
is a positive integer and write its terms in reverse order, we have that

(2-\omega+1)_{k}=(2-\omega+k)(2-\omega+k-1)\cdots(2-\omega+1).

So, this first term corresponds to the power
t^{2-\omega+k}
.

Lastly, we stress that these sums over the complex dimensions
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})
may have delicate convergence properties, as they must be defined as symmetric limits. The use of integration, leading to pointwise formulae for the
antiderivatives
of the relevant tube functions, leads to increased regularity. If we wish to view the formula for the tube function itself, when
k=0
, we may use the distributional formulation in order to do so. We will state the formula when
k=0
and refer the reader to Theorem
3.3.10
for the formulae which are valid for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 3.4.13

(Distributional Tube Formula for GKFs,
k=0
)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for any
\delta>0
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that as an equality of distributions in the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
(the dual of the space defined in Equation
3.3.4
),

V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\Res\Bigg(t^{2-s}\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}(t),

where
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
is given explicitly by Equation
3.4.2
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{2-\varepsilon})
as
t\to 0^{+}
in the sense of Equation
3.3.5
. The action of
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
on a test function is given explicitly by Equation
3.3.7
with
k=0
.

Proof 3.4.14
.

This is a direct corollary of Theorem
3.3.10
with the same minor modification as in the proof of Theorem
3.4.10
. Alternatively, one may first apply Theorem
3.3.10
to the osculant fractal drum
(K,U)
with corresponding self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}
as in Theorem
3.4.1
. Then, one deduces the formula here using the relation that
V_{{K_{n,r}},\omega}(t)=nV_{K,U}(t)
. By linearity of Mellin transforms, it follows that
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta)=n\widetilde{\zeta}_{K,U}(s;\delta)
. Similar holds for the remainder term and its Mellin transform. Lastly, the result follows by the linearity of limits (namely, the symmetric limit defining the summations), of the finite summations within this limit, and of residues (which themselves may be seen as either limits or equivalently as contour integrals).

We conclude with the same corollary which is true in the lattice case, namely that the residues are constants computed directly from the tube zeta function of the relative fractal drum.

Corollary 3.4.15

(Distributional Tube Formula for GKFs,
k=0
)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for any
\delta>0
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that as an equality of distributions in the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
(the dual of the space defined in Equation
3.3.4
),

V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}r_{\omega}\,t^{2-\omega}+\mathcal{R}(t),

where
r_{\omega}
is a constant given by
r_{\omega}:=\Res\left(\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega\right)
, with
\widetilde{\zeta}_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
given explicitly by Equation
3.4.2
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{2-\varepsilon})
as
t\to 0^{+}
in the sense of Equation
3.3.5
. The action of
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
on a test function is given explicitly by Equation
3.3.7
with
k=0
.

Chapter 4
Heat Content of Fractals

In this chapter, we study the
heat content
of regions with self-similar fractal boundaries. On such regions, we consider the
Dirichlet problem for the heat equation
and the total heat within the region as a function of time. The boundary conditions we consider are a model for heat flow into a region, with zero initial temperature inside the region and constant temperature on the boundary.

Our main goal is to demonstrate the role of complex dimensions, which describe the geometric oscillations within a fractal, in this spectral-adjacent context. Our main result is to produce explicit formulae for the heat content, the integral of the temperature function, for small values of the time parameter. This expansion is indexed by the possible complex dimensions of the fractal boundary, with these dimensions controlling the exponents which appear in the expansion. These results constitute partial progress toward understanding the relationship between geometry and spectrum of self-similar fractals in higher dimensions.

Our setting will be the heat equation on a region
\Omega
whose boundary
X
is the attractor of a self-similar system (or the union of such regions placed symmetrically, in the case of generalized von Koch fractals). Given a decomposition of the heat content into the images of
\Omega
under the mappings of the self-similar system, as well as the scaling property of the heat content, we apply the results of Chapter
2
to deduce scaling functional equations and explicit formulae for the heat content. Using the results of
[
vdB00b
]
, we produce explicit results for generalized von Koch fractals.

4.1
Perron-Wiener-Brelot Solutions to the Heat Equation

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a bounded open set. The heat equation with Dirichlet boundary conditions on
\Omega
is the second order, parabolic differential equation given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u-C\Delta u&=0&&\text{in }\Omega\times[0,\infty)\\
u&=f&&\text{on }\Omega\times\left\{0\right\}\\
u&=g&&\text{on }\partial\Omega\times(0,\infty).\end{aligned}\right.

(4.1.1)

Here,
C
is a positive constant called the diffusivity constant. Here, we consider the Dirichlet problem on a cylinder set,
\Omega\times[0,\infty)
, where the spatial region
\Omega
is fixed with respect to the time parameter, for simplicity. However, in order to understand solutions to the heat equation when the boundary of
\Omega
is arbitrary, most notably fractal, we will study the theory of the heat equation on an arbitrary open set
Y
and then specialize to this case to establish scaling functional equations.

To understand the properties of solutions to Problem
4.1.1
, we must discuss the nature of what a solution means first. In particular, we will discuss the notion of a Perron-Wiener-Brelot (PWB) solution, which is suitable for extending the classical theory to arbitrary open sets
Y\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
with boundary conditions imposed on
\partial Y
. While we will later restrict our attention to cylindrical sets when imposing specific boundary conditions, the general theory of PWB solutions does not have this constraint. The advantage of the PWB solution (over, say, a weak solution existing in some corresponding Sobolev space) is that it yields a function which is pointwise defined everywhere in the interior, which is once (in time) or twice (in spatial coordinates) continuously differentiable, and which converges to the prescribed boundary value where possible.

4.1.1
History of the PWB Method for the Heat Equation

Perron introduced his famous method for solving the Dirichlet problem for Laplace’s equation
[
Per23
]
. The basic premise is to obtain the solution, a harmonic function, as a supremum over a family of subharmonic functions. This method was studied by Wiener, who in particular defined a generalized solution as one obtained in this way with limits converging to the prescribed boundary values at regular boundary points and proved a criterion for the convergence of such solutions to the prescribed boundary value conditions
[
Wie24b
,
Wie24
]
.

Wiener’s criterion established existence of such generalized solutions, and the uniqueness of such solutions (namely, that such a solution is identically zero when all its limits approaching regular boundary points are zero) was established by Kellogg
[
Kel28
]
and Evans
[
Eva33
]
. Brelot’s contributions include simplifying this proof
[
Bre55
]
as well as developing a general theory and extending the method (c.f.
[
Bre67
]
). His general study in potential theory was already inspired by others, notably including the work of Doob
[
Doo66
]
. The potential theory would be further developed and axiomatized by Bauer
[
Bau66
]
and Constantinescu and Cornea
[
CC72
]
.

The application of the PWB method to the heat equation relates to the probabilistic approach, owing to the connection of this equation to Brownian motion. This connection was discovered independently by Einstein
[
Ein56
]
and Smoluchowski
[
Smo06
]
. However, some of these same ideas were discovered as early as the work of Bachelier, who studied mathematical finance
[
Bac00
]
. Einstein’s work especially was a large inspiration for Wiener’s mathematical development of the theory of Brownian motion
[
Wie23
,
Wie24a
,
Wie76
]
. It is for this reason that Brownian motion may be instead called a Wiener process.

The analysis of the heat equation on an arbitrary bounded open set in Euclidean space, using the probabilistic approach, is due to Doob
[
Doo55
]
. He obtained the solution in the sense of Perron and Wiener, using the Martingale analogues of subharmonic functions, though he did not provide details for the classification of regular and irregular boundary points. Evans and Gariepy proved an analogue of Wiener’s criterion for the classification of regular and irregular boundary points for solutions of the heat equation
[
EG82
]
. Watson would later show that Doob’s approach is equivalent to the later developments in the theory, including filling in details not originally provided by Doob
[
Wat15
]
. For a modern reference, we recommend Watson’s book on the subject
[
Wat12
]
.

Lastly, we note that the Dirichlet problem for the heat equation requires some extra assumptions in order to be well-posed. (A Dirichlet problem is well-posed if there exists a unique solution which depends continuously on initial conditions. We shall only need concern ourselves with existence and uniqueness in this work, though, having fixed constant boundary conditions.) As was discovered by Tychonoff, functions with growth larger than quadratic exponentials can yield nonunique solutions, but under the restriction to this growth condition solutions are unique
[
Tyc35
]
. Widder further analyzed the problem and showed that nonnegative solutions are unique
[
Wid44
]
. For more information about the (non)uniqueness, we recommend Widder’s paper.

4.1.2
Classes of Temperature Functions

In order to define PWB solutions, we must define a class of
hypertemperatures
(and respectively
hypotemperatures
) to be considered. Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
be an arbitrary open set and we write
p=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
to distinguish the
spatial coordinates

x
and the
time coordinate

t
. Let
C^{2,1}(Y)
denote the class of continuously differentiable functions on
Y
with continuous second partial derivatives with respect to the spatial coordinates (each
x_{k}
,
k=1,...,N
) and one continuous derivative with respect to the time coordinate
t
.

In the simplest case, if we consider
u\in C^{2,1}(Y)
, then we may define the heat operator
1
1

1

Note that we take the opposite convention as that in
[
Wat12
]
, where the heat operator therein is
\Theta:=-\mathcal{H}
.

\mathcal{H}
by
\mathcal{H}u:=\partial_{t}u-\Delta u
, in which case the heat equation is simply
\mathcal{H}u=0
. A
temperature
is a function which is in
C^{2,1}(Y)
and which solves the heat equation, i.e.
\mathcal{H}u=0
.

A
subtemperature
is then a function
u\in C^{2,1}(Y)
such that
\mathcal{H}u\leq 0
. A
supertemperature
is a function such that
\mathcal{H}u\geq 0
. Note that a function
u
is a supertemperature if and only if
-u
is a subtemperature, and in general we need only define one class of functions and we may use this relation to define the other. We see already the theme of the PWB approach: a temperature function is both a subtemperature and a supertemperature.

However, for the study of the heat problem on an arbitrary open set, we will need to consider functions which are not necessarily continuously differentiable. Thus, we will need an analogue of the heat operator that does not require differentiability, as we cannot define
\mathcal{H}
for such functions to consider sub/supertemperatures. There are several equivalent approaches. For instance, one may consider a mean value operator over an appropriate type of set (so-called
heat balls
) and look for functions which agree with their mean values. This is a fundamental property of solutions to the classical heat equation, and can be used to show that such functions obey the heat equation. One may also use a classification in terms of a type of integral called a
Poisson integral
which involves types of measures called
parabolic measures
.

Let us presently take the mean value approach. Let
p_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})
and
r>0
. A
heat ball
is a set of the form

E_{p_{0},r}:=\left\{p=(x,t)\in Y\,\middle|\,|x-x_{0}|\leq\sqrt{2N(t_{0}-t)\log(r/(t_{0}-t))},\,0<t_{0}-r<t<t_{0}\right\}.

(One may represent this set in terms of a lower bound for the
fundamental solution
to the heat equation, hence the name.) We say that a
heat sphere
is the boundary
\partial E_{p_{0},r}
of a heat ball.

In order to avoid the singular behavior which occurs near the center
p_{0}
of a heat ball, we will introduce the function

Q(p)=Q(x,t):=\begin{cases}\cfrac{|x|^{2}}{\sqrt{4|x|^{2}|t|^{2}+(|x|^{2}-2Nt)^{2}}}&(x,t)\neq 0,\\
1&(x,t)=(0,0).\end{cases}

(4.1.2)

Note that
Q
has the property that for any sequence of points
p\in\partial E_{p_{0},r}
converging to
p_{0}
,

Q(p)\to 1
, i.e. it is continuous on
\partial E_{p_{0},r}
.

Definition 4.1.1

(Heat Sphere Mean Value Operator)
.

Letting
p_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}
and
r>0
, consider the heat ball
E_{p_{0},r}
. Let
dS
denote the surface area measure on
\partial E_{p_{0},r}
. Then for any function
u
for which the integral converges, we define the
mean value operator

\mathscr{M}_{p_{0},r}
on
\partial E_{p_{0},r}
to be

\mathscr{M}_{p_{0},r}[u]=\mathscr{M}[u;\partial E_{p_{0},r}]:=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{N/2}}\int_{\partial E_{p_{0},r}}Q(p-p_{0})u(p)dS(p).

(4.1.3)

The last preliminary is to define the properties that our functions must satisfy in lieu of continuous differentiability. We first define the notions of upper and lower
semicontinuity
: a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{N+1}\to[-\infty,\infty]
is
upper semicontinuous
on a set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
if for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
\left\{p\in Y\,\middle|\,f(p)<a\right\}
is a relatively open subset of
S
(i.e. open in the subspace topology it inherits from
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
). We say that an extended real-valued function
f
is
lower semicontinuous
if
-f
is upper semicontinuous.

Next, we say that an extended real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}^{N+1}\to[-\infty,\infty]
is
upper finite
on a set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
if
f[Y]\subseteq[-\infty,\infty)
and
lower finite
on
Y
if
f[Y]\subset(-\infty,\infty]
. Note that when
f
is both upper and lower finite, it is simply a real-valued function.

We may now define hypertemperatures and hypertemperatures.

Definition 4.1.2

(Hypertemperatures)
.

Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
. We say that an extended real-valued function
f
is a
hypertemperature
on
Y
if
f
is lower finite, lower semicontinuous, and with the following property. For any
p\in Y
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
r<\varepsilon
such that

f(p)\geq\mathscr{M}[f;\partial E_{p,r}].

Let
\text{Tem}_{+}(Y)
denote the set of all hypertemperatures on
Y
.

The efficient way to define when a function
f
is a hypotemperature is to require that
-f
be a hypertemperature. However, for the sake of completeness and establishing notation, we will give an independent definition and let this fact be an immediate corollary.

Definition 4.1.3

(Hypotemperatures)
.

Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
. We say that an extended real-valued function
f
is a
hypotemperature
on
Y
if
f
is upper finite, upper semicontinuous, and with the following property. For any
p\in Y
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
r<\varepsilon
such that

f(p)\leq\mathscr{M}[f;\partial E_{p,r}].

Let
\text{Tem}_{-}(Y)
denote the space of all hypotemperatures on
Y
.

Ultimately, when we look for solutions of the heat equation, we will consider the collection of all hypertemperatures and hypotemperatures that satisfy the prescribed boundary conditions. By taking an infimum of hypertemperatures or a supremum of hypotemperatures, then ideally we arrive at the same function which now satisfies the boundary conditions and will be a
temperature
, satisfying the heat equation proper.

4.1.3
Classification of Boundary Points

In what follows, let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
be an arbitrary open set. We consider the boundary of
Y
relative to the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
, so that
\infty\in\partial Y
exactly when
Y
is unbounded. With this proviso,
\partial Y
is otherwise the standard Euclidean boundary of
Y
. For an arbitrary open set
Y
, it turns out that we cannot prescribe boundary conditions on all of
\partial Y
. This is to be
reasonably expected
, however.

Consider the simple problem of a one-dimensional rod given by the interval
[a,b]
and suppose that we wish to consider the problem for time
t\in[0,1]
. Suppose now to look for solutions of the heat equation
\partial_{x}^{2}u=\partial_{t}u
in the set
(a,b)\times(0,1)
. Prescribing conditions on the boundary of
[a,b]\times[0,1]
amounts to three types of constraints. On the set
(a,b)\times\left\{0\right\}
, we prescribe the initial amount of heat on the inside of the rod at time
t=0
. On each of the sets
\left\{a\right\}\times[0,1]
and
\left\{b\right\}\times[0,1]
, we are prescribing the temperature values of the endpoints of the rod. Lastly, on the boundary piece
(a,b)\times\left\{1\right\}
, we are prescribing the future temperature of the inside of rod at time one. However, in this deterministic model, the conditions at time
t=0
and on the boundary for all time
t\in[0,1]
will determine the future temperature; it cannot be arbitrarily chosen.

This leads us to a general classification of the types of boundary points of
\partial Y
. In order to do so, let us establish some notation regarding half-balls with respect to the time coordinate. Letting
p_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
, we write

H_{*}(p_{0},r)=\left\{(x,t)\,\middle|\,|x-x_{0}|^{2}+|t-t_{0}|^{2}<r^{2},\,t<t_{0}\right\}

for the open
lower half-ball
. Similarly, we write
H^{*}(p_{0},r)
for the open
upper half-ball
, defined analogously but with
t>t_{0}
. Note that these are standard balls cut in half, not heat balls.

The boundary
\partial Y
is classified into three types of points:
normal
,
semi-singular
(or
semi-normal
), and
(fully) singular points
. If
q\in\partial Y
, then
q
is said to be
normal
if either
q=\infty
is the point at infinity or if every lower half-ball
H_{*}(q,r)
centered at
q
intersects the complement of
Y
, viz.
H_{*}(q,r)\setminus Y\neq\emptyset
for all
r>0
, namely as
r\to 0^{+}
. For abnormal boundary points
q
, eventually there exists an
r_{0}>0
so that
H_{*}(q,r)\subseteq Y
. If there exists
r_{1}<r_{0}
such that
H^{*}(q,r_{1})\cap Y=\emptyset
, then
q
is a (fully) singular boundary point. (Note that this implies that any smaller upper half-ball
H^{*}(p,r_{2})
,
r_{2}<r_{1}
, will also be contained in the complement of
Y
.) Otherwise, for every
r<r_{0}
we have that
H^{*}(q,r_{1})\cap Y\neq\emptyset
and
q
is said to be
