semi-singular
.

Returning to the example from earlier, we can see now how these criteria describe the different parts of the boundary. The initial condition set,
(a,b)\times\left\{0\right\}
, clearly contains only normal points since the lower half-balls
H_{*}(p,r)
are contained in the complement for any
r
, let alone merely intersecting the complement. On the edges of the rod, we also have that arbitrarily small lower half-balls always intersect the complement to the left or right, respectively. Thus this part of the boundary is also normal.

The top part of the boundary, however, is fully singular: the lower half-balls are strictly contained in the set
Y
and the upper half-balls are strictly contained in the complement. For these fully singular points, they can only be approached from within the set
Y
and only from past times, not future times. The conditions above amount to requiring that fully singular boundary points look locally like such points.

This example does not illustrate a semi-singular point, but for a simple example puncture the square
(a,b)\times(0,1)
at an interior point, say
(x_{0},t_{0})
. This new point in the boundary is then semi-singular. Imposing a boundary condition here amounts to instantaneously changing the temperature on the rod at
x_{0}
at the time
t_{0}
. In this case, one could expect a solution for times
t>t_{0}
which are compatible with the current values of the temperature at
t_{0}
for
x\neq x_{0}
and the new prescribed temperature at
x_{0}
. However, the solution in the larger set will not approach this arbitrarily changed value from times
t<t_{0}
.

So, for the Perron-Wiener-Brelot solutions to follow, we will impose boundary conditions only on the set of normal and semi-singular boundary points. For this reason, we say that the
essential boundary

\partial_{e}Y
of a set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
is the union of the set of all
normal
boundary points, the
normal boundary

\partial_{n}Y
, and the set of all
semi-singular
boundary points, the
semi-singular boundary

\partial_{ss}Y
. If we denote the set of
fully singular
boundary points by
\partial_{s}Y
, called the
singular boundary
, then we have that
\partial_{e}Y=\partial Y\setminus\partial_{s}Y
. Note also that the normal boundary and the semi-singular boundary are disjoint by the definitions of normal and semi-singular boundary points. Recall that
\partial Y
is considered with respect to the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
and that
\infty\in\partial_{n}Y
is always a normal boundary point when
Y
is unbounded.

We now may define the sense in which boundary conditions will be satisfied. We will require convergence of the function to the boundary function at normal points to be for any sequence in
Y
converging to the boundary points. Meanwhile, for convergence to the value of the function at semi-singular boundary points, we will only impose convergence when the sequence approaches from future times, not past times.

Definition 4.1.4

(Regularity and Convergence on the Essential Boundary)
.

Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
be an open set and let
\partial_{e}Y=\partial_{n}Y\uplus\partial_{ss}Y
be its essential boundary. Let
f
be a function on
\partial Y
and
u
a function on
Y
itself.

We say that
u

converges to

f
at a point
q\in\partial_{e}Y
if we have the following.

•

If
q\in\partial_{n}Y
, then we have that for any sequence
\left\{p_{n}\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset Y
with
p_{n}\to q
,
u(p_{n})\to f(q)
.

•

If
q=(y,s)\in\partial_{ss}Y
, then we have that for any sequence
\left\{p_{n}=(x_{n},t_{n})\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset Y
with
x_{n}\to y
and
t_{n}\to s^{+}
,
u(p_{n})\to f(q)
.

When
q\in\partial_{e}Y
is such that these limits exist,
q
is called a
regular boundary point
. We say that
u

converges to

f

on the regular (essential) boundary
if
u
converges to
f
at every regular point
q
in the essential boundary.

The last caveat is that given a set
Y
, it can have both
regular
and
irregular
boundary points in its essential boundary. (A boundary point is called
irregular
if it is not regular in the sense above.) So, the final task is to determine which points in the essential boundary are regular. In the case that the entire essential boundary is not regular, i.e. there exist irregular boundary points, then one must seek to understand which points are regular and which are irregular. We will not explore this issue further here, but refer the reader to the work of Evans and Gariepy
[
EG82
]
or to Section 8.6 of
[
Wat12
]
for more information, including sufficient conditions to ensure that an entire boundary or a specific point in the boundary is regular.

4.1.4
Perron-Wiener-Brelot Solutions

We now have the means to define a Perron-Wiener-Brelot solution to the heat equation on an arbitrary open set. Consider the following Dirichlet problem for the heat equation on an arbitrary open set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
.

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u-C\Delta u&=0&&\text{in }Y,\\
u&=f&&\text{on }\partial Y,\\
\end{aligned}\right.

(4.1.4)

Here,
C>0
is a positive constant.

We consider now two collections of functions which we define based on our boundary function
f
. These are either hypertemperatures bounded from below by
f
or hypotemperatures bounded from above by
f
.

\displaystyle\text{Tem}_{+}(Y;f)

\displaystyle:=\left\{w\in\text{Tem}_{+}(Y)\,\middle|\,\begin{aligned} \liminf_{(x,t)\to(y,s)}w(x,t)&\geq f(y,s)&(x,t)\in Y,\,(y,s)\in\partial_{n}Y\\
\liminf_{(x,t)\to(y,s^{+})}w(x,t)&\geq f(y,s)&(x,t)\in Y,\,(y,s)\in\partial_{ss}Y\end{aligned}\right\},

\displaystyle\text{Tem}_{-}(Y;f)

\displaystyle:=\left\{w\in\text{Tem}_{+}(Y)\,\middle|\,\begin{aligned} \limsup_{(x,t)\to(y,s)}w(x,t)&\leq f(y,s)&(x,t)\in Y,\,(y,s)\in\partial_{n}Y\\
\limsup_{(x,t)\to(y,s^{+})}w(x,t)&\leq f(y,s)&(x,t)\in Y,\,(y,s)\in\partial_{ss}Y\end{aligned}\right\}.

These spaces of functions are each nonempty since
w\equiv\infty
is in the first and
w\equiv-\infty
is in the second.

We next define the upper and lower candidates for the solution to the heat equation.

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{Y,f}^{+}(p)&:=\inf\left\{w(p)\,\middle|\,w\in\text{Tem}_{+}(Y;f)\right\},\\
w_{Y,f}^{-}(p)&:=\sup\left\{w(p)\,\middle|\,w\in\text{Tem}_{-}(Y;f)\right\}.\end{split}

(4.1.5)

These define two functions
w_{Y,f}^{+}
and
w_{Y,f}^{-}
on
Y
. If they agree (and are sufficiently regular), then the function
f
is said to be
resolutive
.

Definition 4.1.5

(Resolutive Function)
.

A function
f
on
\partial_{e}Y
, the essential boundary of
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
, is said to be
resolutive
if the following hold. Firstly, the functions defined by Equation
4.1.5
are the same, i.e.
w_{Y,f}^{+}\equiv w_{Y,f}^{-}
, and in this case, we define
w_{Y,f}:=w_{Y,f}^{+}=w_{Y,f}^{-}
. Secondly, we require that
w_{Y,f}\in C^{2,1}(Y)
.

When the boundary function
f
is resolutive, we obtain a
temperature
function
w_{Y,f}
, as it follows that since
w_{Y,f}\in C^{2,1}(Y)
,
\mathcal{H}w_{Y,f}=0
. From the definitions of the function spaces
\text{Tem}_{+}(Y,f)
and
\text{Tem}_{-}(Y,f)
, note that whenever the limit of
w_{Y,f}
as
p
approaches a point
q
in the essential boundary
\partial_{e}Y
exists, the value of the limit must necessarily equal
f(q)
since
w_{Y,f}\in\text{Tem}_{+}(Y,f)\cap\text{Tem}_{-}(Y,f)
. Therefore,
w_{Y,f}
converges to
f
on the regular essential boundary of
Y
. This is exactly what it means for
w_{Y,f}
to be a solution to a Dirichlet problem for the heat equation in the sense of in the sense of Perron-Wiener-Brelot.

Proposition 4.1.6

(Perron-Wiener-Brelot (PWB) Solution)
.

Let
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
and suppose that
f
is a resolutive function in the sense of Definition
4.1.5
. Then we say that
w_{Y,f}
is the
Perron-Wiener-Brelot solution
to Problem
4.1.4
and we have that
w_{Y,f}
converges to
f
on the regular essential boundary of
Y
in the sense of Definition
4.1.4
.

4.1.5
Parabolic Measure and Resolutive Functions

The final ingredient in our study of PWB solutions is the last but most important remaining question. Given an open set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
, when is a function
f
on
\partial_{e}Y
resolutive? Per Proposition
4.1.6
, this condition is exactly what is required for a PWB solution to exist with the Dirichlet boundary conditions specified by
f
.

Luckily, such functions are plentiful. In fact, any continuous function
f\in C^{0}(\partial_{e}Y)
on the essential boundary is resolutive; see for instance Theorem 8.26 in
[
Wat12
]
. We can give a characterization of the class of resolutive functions in terms of an integrability condition relating to a family of measures called
parabolic measures
.

Given the open set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
and a point
p\in Y
, it can be shown (see Theorem 8.27 in
[
Wat12
]
) that there exists a unique nonnegative Borel probability measure
\omega_{Y,p}
on
\partial_{e}Y
such that for any continuous function
f\in C^{0}(\partial_{e}Y)
,

w_{Y,f}(p)=\int_{\partial_{e}Y}f\,d\omega_{Y,p}.

(4.1.6)

An integral of this form may be called a
Poisson integral
owing to their connection to the classical theory of Poisson kernels and integral representations (in the setting of harmonic functions which can be extended to the heat equation).

Definition 4.1.7

(Parabolic Measure and Parabolic Integrability)
.

The completion of the measure defined by Equation
4.1.6
, which we shall also denote by
\omega_{Y,p}
, is called the
parabolic measure
relative to
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
and
p\in Y
.

A function
f
on
\partial_{e}Y
is said to be
parabolically integrable
if it is
\omega_{Y,p}
-integrable for any
p\in Y
.

The set of resolutive functions
f
for a given set
Y
is exactly the class of
parabolically integrable
functions on
\partial_{e}(Y)
(see for instance Corollary 8.34 of
[
Wat12
]
). The integral representation given by Equation
4.1.6
means that the PWB solution
w_{Y,f}
to Problem
4.1.4
at a point
p
is exactly the parabolic average of the boundary value
f
with respect to the associated parabolic measure
\omega_{Y,p}
.

4.2
General Heat Content Results for Self-Similar Sets

The heat content of a set is the amount of heat within that spatial region, given by an integral of the heat solution over that region as a function of the time variable. In what follows, we will standardize the boundary conditions from Problem
4.1.1
in the following way. First, consider a bounded open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. At the initial time, suppose the total heat inside of
\Omega
is zero and for all time in the future, suppose that the boundary
\partial\Omega
is held at a constant temperature, which we suppose to be equal to one. Explicitly,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u-C\Delta u&=0&&\text{in }\Omega\times[0,\infty),\\
u&=0&&\text{on }\Omega\times\left\{0\right\},\\
u&=1&&\text{on }\partial\Omega\times(0,\infty).\end{aligned}\right.

(4.2.1)

In particular, we will obtain an explicit formula for this quantity for small values of
t
. These boundary conditions are a model for heat flow into a region.

We note that for an arbitrary open set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
, we may use the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
as established in Section
4.1
. The choice of piecewise constant boundary conditions is sufficient to ensure that the boundary function is resolutive. Explicitly, we may define the function

F(x,t)=\mathds{1}_{\partial\Omega}(x),

which is a measurable function for every
\omega_{Y,p}
, with
Y=\Omega\times(0,\infty)
and
p\in Y
, since they are Borel measures and
\partial\Omega
is closed. Further, it is clearly integrable since
\omega_{Y,p}(\partial_{e}Y)=1
since they are probability measures. Thus
F
is parabolically integrable and admits a PWB solution
w_{Y,F}
.

Further, we also have that the solution to Problem
4.2.1
is unique since it is nonnegative, which can be seen from the minimum principle for the heat equation (originally shown in
[
Wid44
]
), or else because it is bounded by the maximum principle, hence certainly of sub-quadratic exponential growth (originally shown in
[
Tyc35
]
).

The choice of constant boundary conditions has another important property: it is
scale invariant
. This is important for establishing the relationship between Problem
4.2.1
and the analogous problem for the set
\varphi[X]
, where
\varphi
is a similitude. In general, the results of this section may be generalized to parabolically integrable, scale-invariant functions.

4.2.1
Heat Content and its Properties

Let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
on the open bounded region
\Omega
. The heat content in
F\subseteq\Omega
, where
F
is a measurable subset of
\Omega
, is simply the integral of
u_{\Omega}
over
F
as a function of the time parameter.

Definition 4.2.1

(Heat Content)
.

Let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution to the Dirichlet Problem
4.1.1
on the set
\Omega
with resolutive boundary conditions. The heat content
E_{\Omega}
inside any measurable set
F\subseteq\Omega
is defined as

E_{\Omega}(t;F):=\int_{F}u_{\Omega}(x,t)\,dx.

(4.2.2)

When
F=\Omega
, then we simply write
E_{\Omega}(t)=E_{\Omega}(t;\Omega)
and call it the
total heat content
. Note that this definition may be stated for the PWB solution for any Dirichlet problem for the heat equation with resolutive boundary conditions, viz. Problem
4.1.4
.

In the case of Problem
4.2.1
, we note that the heat content
E_{\Omega}(t;F)
is uniformly bounded for all
t\in[0,\infty)
and any measurable set
F
.

Proposition 4.2.2

(Heat Content Boundedness)
.

Let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
. Then for any
t\in[0,\infty)
and any measurable set
F\subseteq\Omega
,

|E_{\Omega}(t;F)|\leq\left|\Omega\right|_{N}.

(4.2.3)

Proof 4.2.3
.

This is an immediate corollary of the maximum principle for the heat equation and the boundedness of our chosen set
\Omega
. Firstly, we have that our solution
u_{\Omega}
is nonnegative because
u_{\Omega}(x,0)=0
and the minimum principle. This implies that for any
F\subseteq\Omega
,

0\leq E_{\Omega}(t;F)\leq E_{\Omega}(t;\Omega),

which follows from properties of the Lebesgue measure. Using now the maximum principle for a solution to the heat equation, we have that
|u_{\Omega}(x,t)|\leq 1
, the largest boundary value. Thus,

E_{\Omega}(t;\Omega)\leq\int_{\Omega}dx=\left|X\right|_{N}<\infty.

Next, we deduce a scaling property for the heat content. This will require imposing scale-invariant boundary conditions, the simplest of which are constant boundary conditions.

Proposition 4.2.4

(Heat Content Scaling Property)
.

Let
u_{\Omega}
and
u_{\varphi[\Omega]}
be PWB solutions to Problem
4.1.1
on a bounded open set
\Omega
and
\varphi[\Omega]
in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, respectively, where
\varphi
is a similitude of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with scaling ratio
\lambda>0
. Suppose that
f
and
g
are chosen so that
u_{\Omega}
is unique, e.g. if they are both nonnegative (in which case
u_{\Omega}\geq 0
, which is sufficient by
[
Wid44
]
).

Suppose that the boundary functions
f
and
g
have the following scale invariance properties with respect to
\varphi
: for any
x\in\Omega
,
f
is
scale invariant
(i.e.
f(\varphi(x),0)=f(x,0)
) and
g
is
parabolic scale invariant
(i.e.
g(\varphi(x),\lambda^{2}t)=g(x,t)
). Then

E_{\varphi[\Omega]}(t)=\lambda^{N}E_{\Omega}(t/\lambda^{2}).

Proof 4.2.5
.

First, we have that for any
x\in\Omega
and for all
t>0
,

u_{\varphi[X]}(\varphi(x),\lambda^{2}t)=u_{\Omega}(x,t).

(4.2.4)

To see this, we note that the function
v(x,t):=u_{\varphi[\Omega]}(\varphi(x),\lambda^{2}t)
satisfies the heat equation for any
x\in\Omega
and for all
t>0
, since
(\partial_{t}-\Delta)v=\lambda^{2}(\partial_{t}-\Delta)u_{\varphi[\Omega]}=0
on the points
(\varphi(x),t)\in\varphi[\Omega]\times(0,\infty)
.

Furthermore,
v
satisfies the same boundary conditions as the function
u_{\Omega}
. Let us use the notation in the discussion of Problem
4.1.4
: let
u_{\Omega}=u_{Y,F}
and
u_{\varphi[\Omega]}=u_{\varphi[Y],F}
, where
Y=\Omega\times(0,\infty)
and where
F
represents respective boundary conditions
f
and
g
when appropriately restricted. Let
p_{n}=(x_{n},t_{n})\to q=(y,s)
be a sequence of points in
Y
approaching the boundary point
q
, and in the case of a semi-singular boundary points we assume that
t\to s^{+}
. Let
p^{\prime}=(\varphi(x_{n}),\lambda^{2}t)
and
q^{\prime}=(\varphi(y),\lambda^{2}t)
denote the corresponding points under parabolic transformation.

By assumption, at any regular boundary point
q
(respectively
q^{\prime}
) we have that

\displaystyle u_{Y,F}(p_{n})

\displaystyle\to F(q)

\displaystyle\text{ as }p_{n}\to q\in\partial_{e}Y,

\displaystyle u_{\varphi[Y],F}(p^{\prime}_{n})

\displaystyle\to F(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\text{ as }p_{n}\to q\in\partial_{e}Y.

In other words,
u_{Y,F}
(respectively
u_{\varphi[Y],F}
) converge on the regular essential boundary in the sense of Definition
4.1.4
. We note that if
q
is a regular boundary point, then so too is
q^{\prime}
for its corresponding boundary. This can be seen from the barrier criterion (see for instance Theorem 8.46 of
[
Wat12
]
). Namely, if there is a barrier
w
defined near the point
q
, then the corresponding function
w(\varphi(x),\lambda^{2}t)
will be a barrier at
q^{\prime}
.

By definition,
v(p)=u_{\varphi[Y],F}(p^{\prime})
, so
v(p)\to F(q^{\prime})
. Under the parabolic scale invariance assumption,
F(q^{\prime})=F(q)
. Thus, we conclude by uniqueness that
v(x,t)=u_{\Omega}(x,t)
, which is exactly Equation
4.2.4
.
Using properties of the Lebesgue integral and Equation
4.2.4
, it follows that

\displaystyle E_{\varphi[\Omega]}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}u_{\varphi[\Omega]}(\varphi(x),t)\,d\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{N}\int_{\Omega}u_{\varphi[\Omega]}(\varphi(x),\lambda^{2}(t/\lambda^{2}))\,dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{N}\int_{\Omega}u_{\Omega}(x,t/\lambda^{2})\,dx

\displaystyle=\lambda^{N}E_{\Omega}(t/\lambda^{2}).

Note that an important part of this scaling property is that the heat content in a region is related to a
rescaled
problem’s heat content (c.f.
E_{\lambda\Omega}(t)
), not a
restricted
heat content (c.f.
E_{\Omega}(t;\lambda\Omega)
). Further, we note that Proposition
4.2.4
implies that the total heat content
E_{\Omega}(t)=E(t;\Omega)
viewed as a family of functions satisfies a
2
-scaling law in the sense of Definition
2.4.2
, provided a fixed set of boundary conditions for which Proposition
4.2.4
holds. We state this in our specific case, i.e. for Problem
4.2.1
.

Corollary 4.2.6

(
2
-Scaling Law of Heat Content)
.

Given any bounded open set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
and any similitude
\varphi
on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, let
E_{A}(t)
be the total heat content for the PWB solution
u_{\Omega}
of Problem
4.2.1
for each of the sets
\Omega=A
and
\Omega=\varphi^{\circ k}[A]
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the family of normalized functions
t^{-N/2}E_{A}(t)
(with respect to the
\Phi
-closed set
\mathcal{A}=\left\{\varphi^{k}[A]\,\middle|\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\right\}
) satisfies a
2
-scaling law in the sense of Definition
2.4.2
, viz. for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
t>0
,

t^{-N/2}E_{A}(t/\lambda^{2})=t^{-N/2}E_{\varphi[A]}(t).

Proof 4.2.7
.

The constant, nonnegative boundary conditions of Problem
4.2.1
ensure that
u_{\Omega}
is unique and are clearly scale invariant and parabolically scale invariant in the respective variables. Because
\varphi
is a similitude, every set
\varphi^{\circ k}[A]
is bounded (use the bound
\lambda^{k}C
, where
C
is a bound for
A\subseteq B_{C}(0)
) and open (which follows because
\varphi
is injective and its inverse is also a similitude, hence continuous).

Applying Proposition
4.2.4
to an arbitrary set
A\in\mathcal{A}
, we have that

\displaystyle t^{-N/2}E_{\varphi[A]}(t)=t^{-N/2}\lambda^{N}E_{A}(/\lambda^{2})=(t/\lambda^{2})^{-N/2}E_{A}(t/\lambda^{2}).

Suppose now that our region
\Omega
has a self-similar boundary
X
corresponding to a self-similar system
\Phi
and that
\Omega
is an osculating set for
\Phi
. In order to use the scaling property to obtain a scaling functional equation, we will need an induced decomposition in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
. For later use, we define the remainder which occurs as the difference of the heat content and the sum of scaled copies. Given a suitable estimate of this quantity, we will be able to obtain explicit formulae for the heat content.

Definition 4.2.8

(Decomposition Remainder)
.

Let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
on the bounded open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Suppose that
X=\partial\Omega
is the attractor of a self-similar system
\Phi
and that the relative fractal drum
(X,\Omega)
is osculant.

We define the
decomposition remainder
of
E_{\Omega}
to be the quantity

R_{\Omega}(t):=E_{\Omega}(t)-\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}E_{\varphi[\Omega]}(t).

(4.2.5)

The normalized remainder is the quantity
R(t):=t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)
for
t>0
.

Note that in the case of Problem
4.2.1
, we can deduce that
R_{\Omega}
is bounded for all
t\geq 0
as a corollary of Proposition
4.2.2
. This will provide an upper bound for the abscissa of convergence of its truncated Mellin transform,
\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-N}R_{\Omega}(t)]
, as by Lemma
2.1.2
we may deduce that it is holomorphic in the right half plane
\mathbb{H}_{N/2}
. This helps establish some technical preliminaries for later proofs, allowing us to concern ourselves solely with asymptotic estimates of the form
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

The most precise results occur when there is no error term
R
(in which case we have estimates of the form
O(t^{n})
at
t\to 0^{+}
for any
n>0
), which occurs when the set
X
partitions into disjoint, self-similar copies (up to sets of measure zero). The novelty of this method, though, lies in its ability to handle cases where this is not true, but with explicit estimates for the degree to which the decomposition is not exact, such as in the case of generalized von Koch fractals. The key part of using this scaling function approach is obtaining good estimates for the remainder term (viz. for small values of
\sigma_{0}
), as the expansion will be explicit up to an order determined by these estimates. For the reader wishing to establish such functional equations for a particular class of self-similar sets, we recommend studying the methods and results in
[
FLV95
,
vdB00a
]
.

4.2.2
Heat Zeta Functions

Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a bounded, self-similar set and let
\Phi
be a self-similar system with
\Omega
as its attractor. Suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
is an osculating set for
\Phi
so that
(X,\Omega)
is an osculant fractal drum (see Definition
3.3.1
). Let

X=\left(\biguplus_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega]\right)\uplus{\Omega_{R}}

(4.2.6)

be a partition of
X
where
{\Omega_{R}}
is defined as
{\Omega_{R}}:=X\setminus(\cup_{\varphi\in\Phi}\varphi[\Omega])
. That the union is disjoint follows from the definition of an osculating set.

Our goal is to use the methods of Chapter
2
to study Problem
4.2.1
on the region
\Omega
. If the self-similar system
\Phi
induces a decomposition of the total heat contents (in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
), we can use Corollary
4.2.6
to obtain a scaling functional equation. To this end, we establish now some preliminaries and notation for the application of this theory.

We begin with the notion of a
heat zeta function
, defined in analogy with the definition for tube zeta functions and their normalized quantities which satisfy a scaling law. We define it for the general problem on a cylindrical set and in the case of Problem
4.2.1
, we say that the heat zeta function is a parabolic (heat) zeta function owing to the nature of the solution
u_{\Omega}
as a parabolic average at the points of the given set
\Omega
.

Definition 4.2.9

(Heat Zeta Function)
.

Let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution to Problem
4.1.1
on the open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N+1}
with a resolutive boundary function
F=f\mathds{1}_{t=0}+g\mathds{1}_{\partial\Omega}
. Suppose that the total heat content
E_{\Omega}(t)
associated to
u_{\Omega}
is integrable and bounded for
t\in(0,\delta]
and satisfies the estimate that
E_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Then we define the
heat zeta function
for
s\in\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
to be

\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega,F}(s;\delta):=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-N/2}E_{\Omega}(t)](s)

(4.2.7)

and extended by analytic continuation. If
F=\mathds{1}_{\partial\Omega}
as in Problem
4.2.1
, we say that
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}:=\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega,F}
is a
parabolic
(heat) zeta function.

Note that we have that
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega,F}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
by Lemma
2.1.2
. When we establish functional equations for the heat content induced by a self-similar system
\Phi
, we also note that the relevant scaling operator will be
L_{\Phi}^{2}:=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}M_{\lambda_{\varphi}^{2}}
, but the associated scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
is still the primary function of interest since
\zeta_{\Phi}(2s)=\zeta_{L_{\Phi}^{2}}(s)
. Owing to the effect of this quadratic scaling, viz.
\alpha=2
as in Chapter
2
, we will also state our estimates for the heat content remainders in the form
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}/2})
(as
t\to 0^{+}
). We state our results provided some control over the decomposition remainder as in Definition
4.2.8
.

Theorem 4.2.10

(Formula for the Heat Zeta Function)
.

Let
E_{\Omega}
be the heat content of the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and suppose that
E_{\Omega}
decomposes according to
\Phi
with decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}
(as in Definition
4.2.8
) which satisfies
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Define
\sigma_{R}:=\sigma_{0}/2
and let
\zeta_{\Phi}
be the scaling zeta function associated to
\Phi
. Then for any
\delta>0
and for all
s\in\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
,

\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s;\delta)=\zeta_{\Phi}(2s)(\widehat{\xi}_{\Omega}(s;\delta)+\zeta_{R}(s;\delta)),

(4.2.8)

with
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s;\delta)
meromorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
, having poles contained in a subset of
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}
, the set of poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
. Here,
\zeta_{R}(s;\delta):=\mathcal{M}^{\delta}[t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)](s)
is a holomorphic function in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
and

\widehat{\xi}_{\Omega}(s;\delta):=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{2s}\mathcal{M}_{\delta}^{\delta/\lambda_{\varphi}^{2}}[t^{-N/2}E_{\Omega}(t)](s)

(4.2.9)

is an entire function.

Proof 4.2.11
.

By Corollary
4.2.6
, we have that
E_{\Omega}
satisfies a
2
-scaling law. Together with the presupposed induced decomposition, by Proposition
2.4.3
we have that the normalized
E_{\Omega}
satisfies the scaling functional equation

t^{-N/2}E_{\Omega}(t)=L_{\Phi}^{2}[t^{-N/2}E_{\Omega}(t)]+t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)

(4.2.10)

for any
t\geq 0
. The result is then a corollary of Theorem
2.4.11
, noting that the normalized remainder satisfies
R(t)=t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
at
t\to 0^{+}
, with
\sigma_{R}=\sigma_{0}/2
.

Note that this formula implies that the poles of the heat zeta function
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}=\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}/2}
occur exactly at the points
\omega/2
where
\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
. In Chapter
3
, we see that this set controls the possible complex dimensions of the set
X
relative to its interior
\Omega
. Here, we see that the scaling zeta function
\zeta_{\Phi}
, determined from the scaling ratios and their multiplicities alone, also governs the nature of the zeta functions for self-similar fractals.

As we will use this scaling functional equation in order to obtain explicit formulae in what follows, we record this result as a proposition to which we will refer later.

Proposition 4.2.12

(Heat Scaling Functional Equation)
.

Let
E_{\Omega}
be the heat content of the PWB solution to Problem
4.2.1
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and suppose that
E_{\Omega}
decomposes according to
\Phi
with decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}
(as in Definition
4.2.8
) which satisfies
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Then for all
t\geq 0
, the heat content
E_{\Omega}
satisfies the scaling functional equation given by Equation
4.2.10
. Note that the normalized remainder
R(t)=t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)
satisfies the corresponding estimate
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, where
\sigma_{R}:=\sigma_{0}/2
.

4.2.3
Explicit Heat Content Formulae

We now state some general results regarding pointwise and distributional explicit formulae for the heat contents of open bounded regions
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
whose boundary
X
is a self-similar set and for which
(X,\Omega)
is an osculant fractal drum. The pointwise expansions require integrating the heat content to improve regularity of the function to obtain. Meanwhile, the distributional identities (while less direct) remove this technical hurdle.

First, we state the pointwise identity, given in terms of antiderivatives of the heat content. We denote by
E_{\Omega}^{[k]}
the
k^{\text{th}}
antiderivative of the heat content defined so that
E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(0)=0
. Explicitly, it may be defined by recursion with
E_{\Omega}^{[0]}:=E_{\Omega}
and for
k>0
by

E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(t):=\int_{0}^{t}E_{\Omega}^{[k-1]}(\tau)\,d\tau.

As an additional preliminary to stating the result, we define the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{w}:=\Gamma(z+w)/\Gamma(w)
for
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
. Note that when
w=k
is a positive integer, this simplifies to
(z)_{k}=z(z+1)\cdots(z+k-1)
or, when
w=0
, to
(z)_{0}=1
. Lastly, we note that the summations to follow are defined as symmetric limits of the sums taken over values of
\omega
with bounded imaginary parts for increasingly large upper bounds.

Theorem 4.2.13

(Heat Content, Pointwise Expansion)
.

Let
E_{\Omega}
be the heat content of the PWB solution to Dirichlet Problem
4.2.1
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and suppose that
E_{\Omega}
decomposes according to
\Phi
with decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}
satisfying
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Suppose either that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
(see Definition
2.2.9
) or that the scaling ratios of
\Phi
are arithmetically related (see Definition
1.1.4
).

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\geq 2
and let
\delta>0
. Then for all
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that

E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{(N-s)/2+k}}{((N-s)/2+1)_{k}}\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the remainder satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof 4.2.14
.

By Proposition
4.2.12
, we have that the normalized heat content satisfies a scaling functional equation with respect to the operator
L_{\Phi}^{2}
and remainder
R(t):=t^{-N/2}R_{\Omega}(t)
. Letting
\sigma_{R}=\sigma_{0}/2
, we note that
R(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{R}})
as
t\to 0^{+}
and that
\sigma_{0}<D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
implies that
\sigma_{R}<D_{\ell}/2
. So in the first case, we apply Corollary
2.4.7
to obtain that
R
is an admissible remainder with respect to screens of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon
when
0<\varepsilon<D_{\ell}/2-\sigma_{R}
. Meanwhile, in the lattice case, we apply Theorem
2.4.9
to obtain that any screen of the form
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon
are admissible for all but finitely many
\varepsilon>0
. In either case,
S_{\varepsilon}
is admissible for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon
.

We may now apply Theorem
2.5.1
. Here,
\beta=N
,
\alpha=2
, and
\delta>0
is arbitrary. Note that
N/2\leq\dim_{S}(\Phi)/2
by Theorem
1.3.4
and that
N/2\geq\sigma_{R}
since Proposition
4.2.2
implies that
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in the half-plane
\mathbb{H}_{N/2}
as noted in the discussion following Definition
4.2.8
. Here, with the estimate
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, we obtain by Lemma
2.1.2
that
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
. Note also that
S_{\varepsilon}
is chosen with
S_{\varepsilon}(\tau)\equiv\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon>\sigma_{R}
, so
W_{S_{\varepsilon}}=\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon}
and is contained in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
. Also, we note that for the estimate of the remainder,
\beta/\alpha-\sup(S)+k
in the theorem is
{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2-\varepsilon+k
since
\sup(S)=\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon=\sigma_{0}/2+\varepsilon
and
\beta/\alpha=N/2
.

Lastly, we simplify the sum over
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\alpha W_{S})=\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(2\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon})
. Firstly, note that
2\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}+\varepsilon}=\mathbb{H}_{2\sigma_{R}+2\varepsilon}
. Since
\zeta_{R}
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{R}}
,
\zeta_{R}(s/2)
is holomorphic when
s\in\mathbb{H}_{2\sigma_{R}}=\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
. In both cases, we may choose
\varepsilon
sufficiently small so that
\zeta_{\Phi}(2s)
has no poles of the form
s=\omega
with
\text{Re}(\omega)\in(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}+2\varepsilon)
. In the case when
\sigma_{0}<D_{\ell}
, take
\varepsilon<(D_{\ell}-\sigma_{0})/2
and in the lattice case choose
2\varepsilon
smaller than the distance between
\sigma_{0}
and the closest exceptional point. Then, with the change of variables, we see that when
\text{Re}(s)\in(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}+2\varepsilon)
, the function
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2)
has no poles because
\zeta_{\Phi}(s)
has no poles with
\text{Re}(s)\in(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}+2\varepsilon)
(using Theorem
4.2.10
). Thus the sum over
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}+2\varepsilon})
is the same as over
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
.

The leading order term occurs at the pole
\omega=D:=\dim_{S}(\Phi)
, as this is the abscissa of convergence of
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
. If
D
is a simple pole and the only pole with real part
D
, then the leading order term is explicitly

\frac{1}{((N-D)/2+1)_{k}}\Res(\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);D)\,t^{(N-D)/2+k}.

When the scaling ratios are non-arithmetically related,
D
will be the unique pole with real part
D
; when the scaling ratios are arithmetically related, this will not be true. Instead, the leading order terms will form the Fourier expansion of a periodic function owing to the nature of the structure of the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi}
in this case: they lie on finitely many vertical lines and are periodically spaced. See for instance the proof of Theorem
2.4.9
in this work for an explicit proof of this and more generally we refer the reader to the discussion in Chapters 2 and 3 in
[
LvF13
]
regarding the structure of the complex dimensions of self-similar fractal harps (and their natural generalizations), noting that these zeta functions have the same structure as
\zeta_{\Phi}
, and thus controlling the poles of
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
.

For the distributional setting, the restriction of
k\geq 2
may be relaxed. For these formulae, recall from Chapter
2
that (just as in Chapter 5 of
[
LvF13
]
and Chapter 5 of
[
LRŽ17
]
) we will use as the space of test functions the set of Schwartz functions. These are the functions of rapid decrease near the boundary, given explicitly by

\mathcal{S}(0,\delta):=\Bigg\{\psi\in C^{\infty}(0,\delta)\,\Bigg|\,\begin{aligned} &\forall m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\forall q\in\mathbb{N},\,t^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\\
&\text{and }(t-\delta)^{m}\psi^{(q)}(t)\to 0\text{ as }t\to 0^{+}\end{aligned}\Bigg\}.

(4.2.11)

The tempered distributions are elements of the dual space,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
.

Theorem 4.2.15

(Heat Content, Distributional Expansion)
.

Let
E_{\Omega}
be the heat content of the PWB solution to Dirichlet Problem
4.2.1
. Let
\Phi
be a self-similar system and suppose that
E_{\Omega}
decomposes according to
\Phi
with decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}
satisfying
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2})
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Suppose either that
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
(see Definition
2.2.9
) or that the scaling ratios of
\Phi
are arithmetically related (see Definition
1.1.4
).

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
\delta>0
. Then the heat content, viewed as a tempered distribution, satisfies the identity

\begin{split}E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(t)&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{(N-s)/2+k}}{((N-s)/2+1)_{k}}\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{[k]}(t),\end{split}

(4.2.12)

as
t\to 0^{+}
, where two distributions are equal if they action on an arbitrary test function agrees. See Equation
4.2.13
for the explicit identity of action on test functions. Here,
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. The remainder term, as a distribution, satisfies the estimate that for any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small,
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in the sense of Equation
4.2.14
.

Proof 4.2.16
.

The proof is the same as Theorem
4.2.13
but through application of Theorem
2.5.2
.

To say that Equation
4.2.12
is an identity of distributions means the following. For any test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\begin{split}\langle E_{\Omega}^{[k]},\psi\rangle&=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{\mathcal{M}[\psi]({(N-s)/2+k+1})}{((N-s)/2+1)_{k}}\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2);\omega\Bigg)+\langle\mathcal{R}^{[k]},\psi\rangle.\end{split}

(4.2.13)

The distributional remainder estimate is equivalent to the statement that for all
a>0
and for all
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
,

\Big\langle\mathcal{R}^{[k]}(t),\frac{1}{a}\psi(t/a)\Big\rangle=O(a^{{(N-\sigma_{0})}/2-\varepsilon+k}),

(4.2.14)

as
t\to 0^{+}
. While this formulation is less direct compared to the pointwise expansion, it does have the advantage of requiring less regularity to leverage the expansion. Explicitly, one may set
k=0
and obtain a distributional identity for the heat content itself. Note that in this case, the Pochhammer symbol simplifies to
((N-s)/2+1)_{0}=1
.

4.3
Examples: Generalized von Koch Snowflakes

In this section, we provide a direct application of our results to generalized von Koch fractals. The analysis of the heat content of these fractal snowflakes originates with the work of Fleckinger, Levitin, and Vassiliev
[
FLV95
,
FLV95a
]
on the von Koch snowflake and then the later work of Michiel van den Berg and collaborators
[
vdB00b
,
vdB00a
,
vdBG98
,
vdBH99
]
on generalized von Koch fractals. The main tool in their analysis is the renewal theorem, developed by Feller
[
Fel50
]
in the field of probability.

In so doing, we will recover the results of
[
vdB00b
,
vdB00a
,
FLV95
]
when either
n\geq 5
or in the lattice case. Notably, this extends the results for generalized von Koch fractals to the nonlattice case when
n\geq 5
. Results in the nonlattice case for
n=3
or
4
may be deduced provided a priori knowledge of pole-free regions and estimates for the explicit function
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
with the methods established in this work as well. Furthermore, we explicitly show the role of the complex dimensions in the explicit formulae for the heat content.

4.3.1
Geometric Preliminaries

Given an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake, let
{K_{n,r}}=X
denote its boundary as in Definition
1.4.2
. We will require that
{K_{n,r}}
is a simple, closed curve so that is has a connected interior
\Omega
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
, using the Jordan curve theorem). For this reason, we impose the self-avoidance criterion of Proposition
1.4.3
for
n
and
r
.

In what follows, we will consider Problem
4.2.1
. We let
u_{\Omega}
be the PWB solution and
E_{\Omega}
be the associated heat content. Our goal will be to explicitly describe this heat content in the limit as
t\to 0^{+}
by applying the explicit formulae results. We note that by symmetry, we need only consider the heat content in one portion of
\Omega
contained in a sector of angle
2\pi/n
and that the heat content of the total set is
n
times the heat content in this restricted region.

We let
{\Phi_{n,r}}
be the self-similar system defined by Equation
1.4.2
associated to an
(n,r)
-von Koch curve. We define
\ell=(1-r)/2
to be the conjugate scaling ratio to
r
and we impose that
r\in(0,\frac{1}{3}]
. Note that when
r=\ell=\frac{1}{3}
and
n=3
, this is exactly the standard von Koch snowflake (see Figure
1.6
). The scaling zeta function associated to
{\Phi_{n,r}}
is given explicitly by

\zeta_{{\Phi_{n,r}}}(s)=\cfrac{1}{1-(n-1)r^{2s}-2\ell^{2s}}.

Additionally, we recall some relevant information from Section
3.4
. Letting
D_{\ell}=\underline{\dim}_{S}({\Phi_{n,r}})
, we have that
D_{\ell}>0
when
n\geq 5
,
D_{\ell}=0
when
n=4
, and
D_{\ell}<0
when
n=3
by Lemma
3.4.4
. It is for this reason that our results for nonlattice
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflakes will be restricted to
n\geq 5
since we need this condition to apply the criterion of Theorem
2.4.5
with the available estimates. Meanwhile, in the lattice case (where
n\geq 3
), we have explicit information about the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
. Notably, by Proposition
3.4.6
we have that the poles of
{\Phi_{n,r}}
are all simple.

The last major preliminary is an induced decomposition (in the sense of Definition
2.4.1
) and an estimate of the decomposition remainder term,
R_{\Omega}
. This is needed to find a scaling functional equation for the heat content
E_{\Omega}
. A scaling functional equation for
E_{\Omega}
in the case of
(n,r)
-von Koch fractals was established in
[
vdB00b
]
(without this terminology). The decomposition remainder, which we will denote by
R_{\Omega}
is given explicitly by Equation 1.12 in
[
vdB00b
]
and Proposition 1.2 in
[
vdB00b
]
gives an explicit estimate. Namely,
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t)
as
t\to 0^{+}
, in which case we have that
\sigma_{0}=0
since
N/2=1
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

4.3.2
Explicit Heat Content Formulae

We will give two types of explicit formulae. The first result is regarding the pointwise-valid formulae for
k\geq 2
, and the second will be the distributional formulae specialized to the case when
k=0
. Note that the general pointwise formulae may be deduced in the same way using the general results for heat contents of self-similar sets.

We provide the same results as in Section
3.4
so that the reader may directly compare between the tube formulae and heat content formulae, which are exceedingly similar. Most notably, complex numbers indexing the sums are precisely the same, controlled only by the self-similar system
{\Phi_{n,r}}
. In other words, they depend only on the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell
and their corresponding multiplicities (which depend on
n
).

For the preliminaries regarding the notation and conventions defining the antiderivatives
E_{\Omega}^{[k]}
, the Pochhammer symbol
(z)_{w}
,
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
, and the definition of the sums over complex dimensions as symmetric limits, we refer the reader to Section
4.2
.

Theorem 4.3.1

(Pointwise Heat Content Formulae for GKFs)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
, any
\delta>0
, and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that the antiderivatives
E_{\Omega}^{[k]}
of the heat content
E_{\Omega}
for Problem
4.2.1
on
\Omega
satisfy

E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\Res\Bigg(\cfrac{t^{(2-s)/2+k}}{((2-s)/2+1)_{k}}\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{1-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof 4.3.2
.

We have that
E_{\Omega}
satisfies a scaling functional equation induced by
{\Phi_{n,r}}
with decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t)
as
t\to 0^{+}
by Proposition 1.2 of
[
vdB00b
]
, where
{\Phi_{n,r}}
is as in Equation
1.4.2
. When normalized (noting that
N/2=1
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
), the heat content scaling functional equation takes the form

t^{-1}E_{\Omega}(t)=L_{\Phi_{n,r}}[t^{-1}E_{\Omega}(t)](t)+t^{-1}R_{\Omega}(t).

Further, the remainder
R(t):=t^{-1}R_{\Omega}(t)=O(t^{-\sigma_{0}})
, with
\sigma_{0}=0
, as
t\to 0^{+}
and is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Note that we have also multiplied the expression in
[
vdB00b
]
by
n
(and distributed by linearity) to deduce a scaling functional equation for the total heat content, rather than the amount of heat content contained in a single symmetric sector.

The result then follows from application of Theorem
4.2.13
with the following notes. If we assume that
n\geq 5
, then we have that
\underline{\dim}_{S}({\Phi_{n,r}})>0=\sigma_{0}
by Lemma
3.4.4
. If
n=3
or
n=4
, we have assumed that
r
and
\ell
are arithmetically related.

In the lattice case, we have by Proposition
3.4.6
that the poles of
\zeta_{\Phi_{n,r}}
are simple. Thus, we may simplify the residues to obtain an expansion in powers of
t^{(2-\omega)/2+k}
.

Corollary 4.3.3

(Pointwise Tube Formulae for GKFs, Lattice Case)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that its scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for every
k\geq 2
in
\mathbb{Z}
, any
\delta>0
, and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that the antiderivatives
E_{\Omega}^{[k]}
of the heat content
E_{\Omega}
for Problem
4.2.1
on
\Omega
satisfy

E_{\Omega}^{[k]}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\cfrac{r_{\omega}}{((2-\omega)/2+1)_{k}}\,t^{(2-\omega)/2+k}+\mathcal{R}^{k}(t),

where
r_{\omega}
is a constant given by
r_{\omega}:=\Res\left(\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\right)
, with
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}^{k}(t)=O(t^{1-\varepsilon+k})
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

In order to obtain expansions for the heat content itself when
k=0
, we now move to the distributional formulation of the explicit formulae. We refer the reader to Subsection
4.2.3
for the relevant preliminaries and more information about the action of distributions or the estimates of their remainder terms. The space of test functions is the class of Schwartz functions on
(0,\delta)
,
\mathcal{S}(0,\delta)
, and the tempered distributions are the elements of its dual space,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
.

Theorem 4.3.4

(Distributional Heat Content Formula for GKFs,
k=0
)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that either
n\geq 5
or that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (i.e. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for any
\delta>0
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that as an equality of distributions in the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
(the dual of the space defined in Equation
3.3.4
),

E_{\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}\Res\Bigg(t^{(2-s)/2}\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\Bigg)+\mathcal{R}(t),

where
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{1-\varepsilon})
as
t\to 0^{+}
in the sense of Equation
4.2.14
. The action of
E_{\Omega}
on a test function is given explicitly by Equation
4.2.13
with
k=0
.

Proof 4.3.5
.

The proof is the same as that of Theorem
4.3.1
but with application of Theorem
4.2.15
. Note that we specialize to the case of
k=0
.

When we assume that the scaling ratios are arithmetically related, we may simplify this expression regarding the residues. Namely, we obtain an expansion in powers of
t^{(2-\omega)/2}
with coefficients determined by the residues of
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
at the poles
\omega
.

Corollary 4.3.6

(Distributional Tube Formula for GKFs,
k=0
)
.

Let
{K_{n,r}}
be an
(n,r)
-von Koch Snowflake boundary satisfying the self-avoidance criterion in Proposition
1.4.3
and let
\Omega
be the interior region defined by
{K_{n,r}}
(i.e. the bounded component of
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus{K_{n,r}}
). Suppose that the scaling ratios
r
and
\ell=(1-r)/2
are arithmetically related (viz. the ratio of their logarithms is rational).

Then for any
\delta>0
and every
t\in(0,\delta)
, we have that as an equality of distributions in the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(0,\delta)
(the dual of the space defined in Equation
3.3.4
),

E_{\Omega}(t)=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi_{n,r}}(\mathbb{H}_{0})}r_{\omega}\,t^{(2-\omega)/2}+\mathcal{R}(t),

where
r_{\omega}
is a constant given by
r_{\omega}:=\Res\left(\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s/2;\delta);\omega\right)
, with
\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}
is as in Theorem
4.2.10
. For any
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, the error term satisfies
\mathcal{R}(t)=O(t^{1-\varepsilon})
as
t\to 0^{+}
in the sense of Equation
4.2.14
. The action of
V_{{K_{n,r}},\Omega}
on a test function is given explicitly by Equation
4.2.13
with
k=0
.

Conclusion

The thesis of this work is that self-similar fractals have complex dimensions controlled by the scaling ratios in their construction and furthermore that these complex dimensions govern both geometric and spectral properties of these fractals. On the geometric side, we have analyzed tube functions and the possible complex dimensions (Chapter
3
) and on the spectral side, we have analyzed the heat content of self-similar fractals (Chapter
4
). The connection is made evident through the shared method of proof, namely by the means of establishing and solving scaling functional equations (Chapter
2
).

To conclude this work, we now compare our results in these separate chapters to see the the connections between these quantities for self-similar fractals. Then, we broaden our scope to the context in which our results fit and the questions or programs that they fall under. Finally, we leave the reader with new questions arising from our results to complement the questions which began our journey and may yet lead to new inspiration.

Discussion of the Thesis

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
be a bounded open set with fractal boundary
\partial\Omega
. Suppose that
\partial\Omega
is a self-similar set, arising as the attractor of the self-similar system
\Phi
, and suppose that
(\partial\Omega,\Omega)
is an osculant fractal drum with respect to
\Phi
. (In the notation of Chapter
3
,
X=\partial\Omega
.) Per Definition
3.3.1
, this means that
\Phi
satisfies the open set condition with
\Omega
as a feasible open set and that the osculating condition holds for
\Omega
. For explicit examples of such fractals, one may consider when
\Omega
is the interior of an
(n,r)
-von Koch snowflake and
\partial\Omega
is the snowflake boundary.

Lastly, we assume some admissibility conditions. Given the respective remainder functions
V_{\partial\Omega,R}
(i.e. the tube function of the residual set as defined in Theorem
3.3.2
) and the decomposition remainder
R_{\Omega}
(as in Definition
4.2.8
), let
\sigma_{0}
be such that as
t\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle V_{\partial\Omega,R}(t)

\displaystyle=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}}),

\displaystyle R_{\Omega}(t)

\displaystyle=O(t^{(N-\sigma_{0})/2}).

Note that we have chosen
\rho=N-\sigma_{0}
in the notation of Chapter
4
for this comparison. We suppose that either
\sigma_{0}<\underline{\dim}_{S}(\Phi)
or that the scaling ratios of the similitudes in
\Phi
are arithmetically related.

Under these assumptions, one may apply any of the corresponding pairs of theorems from Section
3.3
and Section
4.2
for tube formulae or for heat content, respectively, either for pointwise or distributional asymptotic expansions. For the purposes of this discussion, let us view the distributional expansions with
k=0
and suppose that the poles of the function
\zeta_{\Phi}
are simple, such as for generalized von Koch fractals in the lattice case. Applying Corollary
2.5.3
and Theorem
4.2.15
(with
k=0
), we obtain the following distributional identities:

\displaystyle V_{\partial\Omega,\Omega}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res(\widetilde{\zeta}_{\partial\Omega,\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega)\,t^{N-\omega}+\mathcal{R}_{V}(t);

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle E_{\Omega}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})}\Res(\widehat{\zeta}_{\Omega}(s;\delta);\omega)\,t^{(N-\omega)/2}+\mathcal{R}_{E}(t),

(4.3.2)

where
\mathcal{R}_{V}(t)=O(t^{N-\sigma_{0}-\varepsilon})
and
\mathcal{R}_{E}(t^{(N-\sigma_{0})/2-\varepsilon})
as
t\to 0^{+}
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.

Observe that Equation
4.3.1
and Equation
4.3.2
are strikingly similar. In both cases, there are constants
a_{\omega}
or
b_{\omega}
, given by the residues of the respective zeta function at
\omega
, and monomials of the form
t^{N-\omega}
or
t^{(N-\omega)/2}
, with the complex numbers
\omega
indexed by the set
\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})
. By Corollary
3.3.7
, this set is exactly the possible complex dimensions of the RFD
(\partial\Omega,\Omega)
in
\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}
. So, the complex dimensions of the fractal directly determine the form of the expansion.

Further, we have that the possible complex dimensions are computable directly from knowledge of the self-similar system
\Phi
alone. Namely, by Corollary
3.3.7
, we have that

\mathcal{D}_{\Phi}(\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}})=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{H}_{\sigma_{0}}\,\middle|\,1=\sum_{\varphi\in\Phi}\lambda_{\varphi}^{\omega}\right\}.

It is through this knowledge of the from of
\zeta_{\Phi}
that we can plot possible complex dimensions such as in Figure
3.6
for generalized von Koch fractals. For self-similar fractals in any dimension, provided the osculant separation conditions, we can thus see that the possible complex dimensions are exactly the expected complex dimensions prescribed by Moran’s equation.

Results in Context

This computation of the possible complex dimensions is a partial resolution of Conjecture 6.2.36 of
[
LRŽ17
]
, as we have shown that self-similar fractals with appropriate separation conditions have complex dimensions controlled by the complexified Moran equation. It remains to establish exactly when the possible complex dimensions are the actual complex dimensions, which hinges on computation of the residues of the tube zeta function of
(\partial\Omega,\Omega)
. The computation of these residues, as well as the residues of the heat zeta function, is perhaps the most important follow-up question for future consideration. Not only will it answer the question of when the possible is actual, but also it allows one to use the explicit formulae developed herein numerically rather than only qualitatively.

This work also serves to generalize the known results regarding heat asymptotics of generalized von Koch fractals, from the results of Fleckinger, Levitin, and Vassiliev on the von Koch snowflake
[
FLV95
]
to the work of van den Berg on generalized von Koch fractals
[
vdB00b
]
. Theorem
4.3.1
, Corollary
4.3.3
, Theorem
4.3.4
, and Corollary
4.3.6
all give different types of explicit formulae for the heat content of GKFs in the nonlattice case when
n\geq 5
, extending the results of the lattice case found in these works. Furthermore, the results of Section
4.2
give a general method for computing explicit formulae directly from estimating decomposition remainders.

In regards to tube formulae, this work extends the results of Lapidus and Pearse regarding the von Koch snowflake
[
LP06
]
to its generalized analogues with different parameter values of
n
and
r
. Explicitly, Theorem
3.4.8
, which originally appeared in
[
Hof25
]
, gives a characterization of the possible complex dimensions of GKFs. Theorem
3.4.10
, Corollary
3.4.12
, Theorem
3.4.13
, and Corollary
3.4.15
all give explicit formulae for the tube functions based on the knowledge of these possible complex dimensions. The results of Section
3.3
generalize the work of
[
DKÖÜ15
]
to establish tube formulae from approximate functional equations which contain remainder terms. Many more examples of fractals may be readily approached by the results of Chapter
3
, for example the study of the Menger sponges and its generalizations which is underway.

In the general setting, the results of Chapter
2
establish means by which to solve a general class of scaling functional equations. Section
2.4
, notably, makes precise the assumptions on the remainder term which are required for this process and establishes several sufficient criteria for admissibility. This fills in the gaps for using this method, originally explored in
[
Hof25
]
for application to generalized von Koch fractals, for the general setting. Notably, we have corrected an error in the statement of Theorem 6.3 of
[
Hof25
]
and have introduced terminology which makes precise the missing assumption, namely the notion of joint languidity (Definition
2.3.8
). (We note that Theorem 6.3 is still valid under the assumption that the remainder zeta function is strongly languid, and in fact the proof of its strong languidity, bypassing the need for these extra assumptions, will be the subject of a future work.) Further, we have proven sufficient conditions for this new assumption to hold in the general case (see Theorem
2.4.5
, Corollary
2.4.7
, and Theorem
2.4.9
).

Questions for Future Study

A major through line of this work is the question of how geometry and spectra are related. This study is related to Conjecture 6.2.36 in
[
LRŽ17
]
, which is in a sense an entire program. In this work, we have focused on two types of quantities: volumes of tubular neighborhoods and the heat content of self-similar fractals. Already, these results suggest a strong connection between the geometric complex dimensions of fractals in higher dimensions and quantities of spectra. The connection between geometry and spectra for bounded open sets in
\mathbb{R}
has been established in
[
LvF13
]
, and this work suggests that similar will be true at least for self-similar fractals.

However, it is likely to be true for many more, if not all, fractals in higher dimensions that quantities of geometry and spectra are strongly related to complex dimensions. The results herein suggest that for fractals with underlying dynamics (here, fractals which are attractors of self-similar systems), there is strong reason to believe that the properties of the dynamics will control both geometric and spectral properties of the set. Establishing functional equations, even perhaps infinite or nonlinear functional equations, may be the key to extending this connection from the self-similar case.

Beyond heat and volume, it stands to reason that for self-similar fractals the spectrum of the Laplacian on the fractals should be computable. Indeed, we conjecture that the results of Lapidus and Kigami regarding the spectra of post critically finite (p.c.f) fractals
[
KL93
]
, which uses the method of Neumann-bracketing, should extend to self-similar fractals which form osculant fractal drums. These p.c.f. fractals may only have finitely many points of overlap between different images, but osculant fractal drums can have infinitely many points of overlap (but with Lebesgue measure zero).

Further, these types of fractals allows one to study regions with fractal boundary, such as von Koch snowflakes. If the method of Neumann bracketing can be adapted to produce scaling functional equations for the spectral counting function, then the results of Section
2.5
would produce explicit formulae for the spectrum and its counting function. Such a method could be an approach to extending the results of Lapidus regarding the modified Weyl-Berry conjecture
[
Lap91
]
to obtain asymptotic expansions for regions with self-similar fractal boundary.

Lastly, our work on the heat content in Chapter
4
suggests several approachable problems with generalize upon our results. The explicit formulae, combined with explicit computations or numerical estimates of the residues of heat zeta functions, can lead to finding explicit self-similar fractals which optimize heat flow into (or out of) a region with fractal boundary.

Alternatively, the particular choice of boundary conditions considered may be generalized. The general PWB solution to the Dirichlet problem allows for a wide variety of functions as boundary conditions, including even a time-dependent boundary of the set itself. For example, were an actual snowflake melting, its boundary would shrink over time. So, the models of fractal snowflakes (such as GKFs) could be adapted to having time-dependent boundaries. However, the convergence of the solution to the boundary values, vis a vis classifying the regular and irregular boundary points and resolutive initial conditions, would need to be analyzed carefully. Also, in order to adapt the methods of this work to such situations one would need to develop a theory of time dependence of the self-similar system and perhaps restrict to boundaries which change in a uniform or self-similar way.

Beyond the heat equation, it is natural to wonder whether these same techniques apply to a general parabolic differential equation on self-similar fractals or regions with self-similar fractal boundary. Indeed, if one wishes to study a more complicated type of interaction such as convection in a rough material (viz. diffusion and advection in a region with fractal boundary) one would be led to such problems. Restricting to linear equations would be most compatible with the method of self-similar partitioning, however some form of equivariance may be appropriate for the behavior of solutions under similitude mappings.
